LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by
Townsend on the north, Shirley on the east,
Leominster and Lancaster on the south, and
Fitchburg and Ashby on the west. It is about
47 miles from Boston and 29 miles from Worcester.

Board of Selectmen
"Progressive Action"
NORMAND THOMPSON
RUDOLPH MASELLA
F. CLIFTON MILLER
Board of Selectmen: left to right - F. Clifton Miller, Chairman Rudolph Masella and Normand J. Thompson.
The year 1971 has been one of the most demanding ever encountered by the Board of Selectmen. Progressive
action which took place in 1971 will be reflected not only in 1972 but for many years to come. The Board of Select-
men is responsible for the operational as well as financial management of many Town departments. We are very for-
tunate in Lunenburg tohave cooperative, dedicated and responsible individuals not only as department heads but also
as employees working in these departments. This close-working relationship, thus assured in Lunenburg, is not always
true in towns our size.
Progress was made in two important categories this year--changes in our refuse disposal area and proposed re-
building of Route 2A necessitated by the high accident rate. One of the major responsibilities of the Board of Health
is the operation of the Municipal Refuse Disposal Area on Townsend Harbor Road. In order to comply with the "No
Burning" law of the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, a controlled disposal area was initiated by the Board of Health,
an Attendant was hired and specific hours were established. The current condition of the disposal area is testimony
to the success of the program. A Refuse Disposal Planning Committee was appointed in 1971 to study the critical
refuse disposal problem and it is hoped that a recommended long-range solution will be ready for submission to the
Town at the 1972 Town Meeting.
Anotherarea of prime concern to the Board of Selectmen is public safety. For many years the Board of Select-
men has been disturbed with the high accident rate and fatalities on Route 2A and has been urging the Department
of Public Works and State officials to take corrective action. All possible avenues to correct this situation have been
examined and with the continued increase in motor vehicle traffic, the problem continued to escalate. After many
meetings with the State Department of Public Works the only course of action left was the relocation and rebuilding
of Route 2A which is scheduled to start this spring.
- 4 -
===PAGE 7===
The Board of Selectmen took the necessary action to become one of the first communities in the area to employ
persons under the provisions of the Emergency Employment Act of 1971. This Act provides that all wages and fringe
benefits which would normally be paid by a community are paid by the Federal Government. At present, two regular
policemen and the Attendant at the Municipal Refuse Disposal Area are employed under this program which, as of
December 31, 1971, has amounted to approximately $3,556.43 in federal funds for a two and one-half month period.
The Town is at present awaiting final approval to employ a Fire/Police Radio Dispatcher under this program, which,
if granted, would indicate that approximately $25,000.00 will be received by the Town for those employed under
the Emergency Employment Act during 1972.
Board of Assessors
Board of Assessors: John F. Nicoll, Clerk Kathleen M. Deming, Bernard J. Paton and Chairman Leroy Allen.
The year 1971 was the second year in which Lunenburg was at full-fair valuation. In 1970, under full-fair
valuation, the tax rate was $33.00 per thousand. Due to a decrease in state receipts and a substantial increase in
town appropriations, the tax rate for 1971 rose to $42.50, an increase of $9.50 per thousand. The local taxpayers
are reminded thatas we expand and increase local services, we must expect a comparable increase in the tax rate.
The Assessorshave made an attempt to correct any inequities or differences reported by the local taxpayers, Consider-
able time has been spent reviewing properties where there were questions and listing new buildings constructed in
town this past year.
The Assessors attended several State and County Association meetings to keep themselves informed on new
legislation. The State Tax Commissioner, recognizing the difficulties of part-time assessors, has determined that all
assessors in the State must attend a school set up by the Commissioner. A manual is alsobeing prepared for assessors
to assist them in making more uniform appraisals throughout the Commonwealth.
Mr. Chester J. Pliska, a member of the Board of Assessors for the last seventeen years, did not seekre-election
in 1971. The Assessors wish to extend their thanks to "Chet" for his many years of service to the community.
- 5 -
===PAGE 8===
Town Clerk, Tax Collector, Treasurer
There is a limit to what is sane and responsible in Taxation, to the price
people are willing andable to pay. The majority believe that this limit has been
reached, for throughout the State and the Nation the cost of providing basic ser-
vices to the people has skyrocketed, forced upward by inflation, the rising expec-
tations of a growing population, and increased wage levels for public employees.
It is estimated that the cost of state and local services isannually increasing three
times as fast the income from existing state and local taxes. Unless substantial
relief, suchas revenue sharing with the Federal and State governments, materializes
in the near future, the only alternative to rising taxes would seem to be a change in
public expectations in such fields as education, and curtailment of basic services.
More than 80% of state and local revenue comes from sales, property and ex-
cise taxes. This year in Lunenburg 93.5% of all monies received came from state
Mary L. Padula
and local taxes, 21.8% from the State, 64.5% from local property taxes, and 7.2%
from motor vehicle excise. It has been estimated that of the 53 billion dollar in-
crease in state-local tax yields from 1955 to 1969, less than 2/3 came from growth. In Lunenburg, an even smaller
portion, 42%, was attributable to growth, the balance being absorbed by existing property owners. In that period,
while our population increased 44%, the assessed value of all real and personal property increased 77%, property
taxes increased 317%, and the average homeowner found his tax bill to be 2 1/2 times what it was 15 years before.
From 1955 to 1969, state and local expenditures rose 244%, while Lunenburg's expenditures increased 337%.
Economists have estimated that during this period inflation accounted for 44% of the increase in state and local spend-
ing, rising work loads for 26%, and increased scope and quality of services for 30%. Exactly 70% of all amounts
spent for services in Lunenburg in 1971 was for school purposes. In Massachusetts, approximately 72% of school
costs are paid from property taxes, with State and Federal governments paying the remaining 28%. In 1960 Amer-
icans spent 25 billion on public education, and by 1970 that amount had doubled. In the same period, Lunenburg's
expenditures for education rose 21/2 times.
The following chart calls attention to the discordance between population and enrollment trends and increases
in expenditures, for whereas our growth rate has stabilized and school enrollments have commenced to decline, ex-
penditure curves continue upward at an alarming rate, and show no signs of levelling off.

A comparison of total earnings records for the calendar years 1970 and 1971 reflects average increases for full
time municipal employees as follows: Non-School Administrative and Professional - 7.4%; Non-School Non-Admin-
istrative - 7.7%; School Teachers and Administrators - 13%; School Clerical - 9.3%; School Custodial - 10.6%.
Non-school wages, which are controlled by a Salary Administration Plan voted by the Town Meeting and administered
by a Personnel Board, in 1971 totalled $309, 408. Wages paid to school personnel totalled $1,609,850. Although
our school teachers have the power of collective bargaining, they do not have the right to strike, and their wage
levels are therefore subject to approval of the voters, which says in effect that the fiscal autonomy of the School
Committee is still their most effective bargaining tool.
Detailedrecords of the Town Clerk, Tax Collector and Treasurer may be found elsewhere in this book. Of par-
ticular interest was the registration as voters of 139 18 and 19 year olds. This total represents approximately 4% of
our total voter registration. Fees received by the Town Clerk in 1971 totalled $1,596.88.

===PAGE 9===
Planning Board
"Rules & Regulations" "
Amendments to the "Rules and Regulations Governing the Sub-division of Land" are planned to require utilities
to be placed underground in new sub-divisions. Increased fees for inspections will be required as well as certain
other charges that experience has proven to be desirable. A new issue of the Protective By-Law is being printed
incorporating all changes to date and arranged in a format which we believe will be more convenient to use.
Revisions to the Protective By-Law were enacted at the 1971 Town Meeting. These revisions require that pro-
visions for parking be included in plans submitted for building permits. Also, a section on definitions was added and
penalties specified for violations of the Protective By-Law.
The Board is concerned with potential sewerage problems and future supply of pure water for the Town as land
development continues. Steps are being taken to take advantage of monies that now appear to be available for a
comprehensive study of the sewerage needs of the Town. The development of land in Lunenburg has continued at an
accelerating pace in 1971. The second section of the Broadmeadow development is in progress and definitive plans
for three other sub-divisions have been received. A preliminary plan has been received for a 249 lot sub-division
in the Pleasant Street-Electric Avenue area. In addition, plans for about 30 lots involving division of parcels "not
requiring sub-division approval" have been signed by the Board as required by statute.
The Planning Board has had inquiries from two developers who wish to build multi-unit housing. The Board
participated in hearings before the Board of Appeals regarding an appeal involving low and moderate income housing
under the General Laws, Chapter 40B. With the advent of increased development in the Town, the Board will be
reviewing the present zoning and sub-division by-laws in an attempt to determine whether or not modification should
be made to the By-Law in order to provide for more orderly future growth, open space and retention of the pleasing
evironment of our Town. The Planning Board will welcome suggestions from the citizens of Lunenburg.
0000
Planning Board: Left to right -Harold D. Harley, William A. Mason, Jr.,
Seymour L. Schnuer, Chairman Paul J. Stone and Charles J. Darling.

Park Commission
"Giant Steps"
This was a year of giant steps for the Park Com-
mission and for the Townspeople of Lunenburg. The
Commission received its first new dump truck which
has permitted the department to be far more efficient
and less dependent upon other departments to get
the job done. The Park Commission finally has its
"Own Home", asection of the Old Town Garage on
West Street, thus enabling the storage of benches,
bleachers, tables, equipment, and the repairing and
maintaining of equipment during the winter.
Lunenburg now has more people involved in re-
creational activities than ever before. This has been
accomplished with very little increase in the budget
except for basic salary increases.
Teenage boys and girls basketball (non-team
playing) was conducted last winter on a six-week
pilot program and proved so successful that a ten-
week programwill be conducted this year with spon-
sors and uniforms. Presently there are forty high
school boys and twenty-two high school girls en-
rolled in this program.
A "Street Hockey" program, as part of the
Summer Recreational Program, was initiated at Wal-
lis Park and should be a big step in preparing our
youth for regular ice hockey. The Summer Recrea-
tional Program at Wallis Park, the Center Park and
Normand Park, under the direction of Ray Okerman,
was very successful again this year. This was largely
Park Commissioners: left to right - Robert J. Okerman,
due to the efforts of the very capable supervisors
Chairman Almon O. Ruggles and Lance D. May.
working at these parks, Linda Tucker, Donna Caul-
field and Randy Underwood. An innovation this
year was that prior to the opening of the summer season, all supervisors attended a Recreation Supervisory Clinic
in Leominster. It should be noted that attendance at the Center Park and Wallis Park was above average this past
year, but very disappointing at Normand Park.
One of the important events of the playground season was a Carnival for the benefit of Muscular Dystrophy.
This Carnival, put on by all the playgrounds, was backed by the Townspeople and a $70.00 profit realized from the
joint effort. Among other features of the summer program were trips to Benson's Animal Farm, Willard Brook State
Park, many trips to the Town Beach, two children's films shown at the High School and occasional bicycle trips and
nature hikes.
Not much success has been met in organizing a girls' summer softball team and a men's horseshoe league. It
is that these goals could have been attained if enough people had shown interest in these programs and expressed
their feelings on same. Junior Hockey has been "launched" in Lunenburg. Both Peewee and Bantam League teams
were started and the Park Commissioners are pleased with the initial results. Junior basketball, under the direction
of Dick Bishop, has been expanded with two extra farm teams for a total of 120 boys involved--an all-timehigh for
the program.
- 8 -
===PAGE 11===
The Arts and Crafts program was ably conducted under the leadership of Marianne Sanderson, aided by Elizabeth
Kevilus. For the first time the Red Cross-sponsored summer swimming program, under the direction of Gerry Beloin,
surpassed that of Fitchburg with 471 Lunenburg children enrolled compared to their 420. The Park Commissioners
wish to thank the Lunenburg Red Cross for their financial backing for this program. With surplus funds, a new roof
was installed at the Town Beach and should help preserve this investment.
Civic-minded Lunenburg residents did a tremendous job in repairing the "Old Band Stand" which now appears
to be safe and well preserved. The new Band Shell on the school grounds was dedicated this year and turned over
to the School Department. The Park Commission is very pleased with the tennis program which had David Johnston
as instructor. The winter tennis program has been very successful and will continue at the High School gymnasium.
Adult Physical Education was expanded and was very popular with Lunenburg graduates who spent their leisure
hours on Wednesday evenings rticipating in these activities. A Young Men's Basketball program isnow underway
on Wednesday evenings also. The Proctor Park Ski Area access excavation has been completed but it is felt that fur-
ther expenditures in pursuit of a ski area at this site would not be in the best interests of the Town of Lunenburg at
this time. A safety fence was installed at Normand Park with funds voted at the last Town Meeting. Vandalism has
been a problem again this year.
Initial plans for next year call for repair of the tennis courts at Marshall Park, a new heating system to replace
the defunct one at the Old Town Garage on West Street, an overhead door for the same building and a raft at the
Town Beach. Also contemplated is an ice skating rink. The Park Commission is continually striving to improve ex-
isting programs.
Tree Warden
During 1971, 152 diseased trees were cut down and burned. The Department of Natural Resources gave three
days of sampling amounting to $150.00, and ten days of work amounting to $1,000.00, to assist in detection and re-
moval of diseased trees. Approximately 27 samples were takenby the State and sent to the Shade Tree Laboratory,
University of Massachusetts, for culture. A total of $6,503.15, of which $324.15 was reimbursed by the County,
was spent under the Dutch Elm Disease Account.
Tent Caterpillars infest wild cherry, wild apple, old orchards and shag bark hickory trees and are seen in early
spring. They emerge from their tent and feed all over the tree.
Fall Webworms are similar in type and habits and also infest the same trees. These pests feed only in the con-
fines of their web. Neither does any damage to well cared for trees.
Fifteen Norway Maples were purchased to replace diseased trees with Tree Warden funds. A total of $3,677.00
was spent under this account.
The "Gypsy Moth "-When a single tree or a small number of trees is attacked by gypsy moths, a homeowner may
wish to apply the following techniques developed many years ago, but which can still be quite effective. 1. Tie
with heavy twine, a ring of burlap (about 1 foot wide) around the tree at about breast height. Let the top half of
the burlap hang down over the bottom half. During their later instars, the large caterpillars seek the burlap folds
for protection during the heat of the day. They can be collected daily and destroyed. 2. The egg masses can be
painted with creosote any time prior to hatching. This will effectively kill the eggs. However, caution should be
exercised to prevent exposure of the skin to this material as it is an established cancer producing agent. Creosote
will also kill one of the important gypsy moth egg parasites if it is present in the egg mass.

Library Trustees
Library Trustees: left toright - Beatrice M. Fay, Robert S. Tapply, Library Manager Jean Tenander, Janet J. Cragin
and Chairman Carey D. Rhoten. Absent when picture was takenwere Jeanne L. Crocker and George R. Wallace 3rd.
The year 1971 was a year of change for the library. For the first half of the year the library operated without
a Library Manager. Mrs. Ruth Proctor, Assistant Librarian, undertook the job of Acting Library Manager as well as
continuing her own work. During that time, the Library Trustees, unable to prevail upon Mrs. Proctor to assume the
job, looked for a new Library Manager. In September, Miss Jean Tenander was hired. Mrs. Proctor then resumed
her job as Assistant Librarian. Miss Tenander, who is from Fitchburg, has an M.L.S., an M.A. in Comparative Lit-
erature and a B.A. from the University of Michigan.
The second new staff member at the library is Mrs. Emily Foster, a Lunenburg resident, who began work in Jan-
vary and, since September, has been working in the children's section. Mrs. Foster is the person most frequently seen
at the check-out desk.
A filmprogram was started at the library in late December and, although just underway at this writing, hope-
fully it will prove to be a success. The films to be shown are both American and foreign and are selected either be-
cause they are artistic successes, or they represent a certain period in filmmaking, or because they are the works of
famous directors. The films are shown on Sunday evenings for adults and high school age people. A separate children's
film program may start sometime next year.
- 10
===PAGE 13===
Story Hour continued this year with Mrs. William J. Howe as the reader from January until June. From September
until mid-December Peter Steinberger, Miss Tenander's husband, read to the children. Mrs. Foster is now the reader
for the Story Hour which is held every first and third Friday of the month at 10:30 in the morning. Children fromthree
to five years old are invited.
In October, the Library Trustees held an informal tea for the new Library Manager. Special invitations were
sent out to members of the School Department and to Town officials and the public was, of course, also invited.
Total circulation figures increased from 43,275 to 50,063. The number of adult books circulated was 19,223,
an increase of 3,034 from last year. Children's book circulation increased from 25,388 to 29,008. Magazine circu-
lation increased from 1,698 to 1,832.
In June, the library began to issue new cards to borrowers and to date has issued a total of 1,761 cards. Prior
to this 5,623 people were on file as card holders and users of the library. However, since the town population is
approximately 7,500 this suggested that virtually everyone in town had a library card. It was for this reason the
decision was made to issue new cards as a way of eliminating the hundreds of names of people who had left town,
died, or for some reason no longer made use of the library.
The number of books purchased this year suffered a marked reduction from last year but this is due solely to the
fact that because of the lack of staff, the library operated on a reduced book budget and the staff concentrated on
readers' services. Adult books purchased this year totaled 275 and children's books, 170. Ninety-seven adult books
and 160 children's books were discarded and are on sale at the library for 10 cents each.
The Regional Library System's bookmobil stopped at the library once a month with a total of 581 books borrowed
this year. One hundred seventy-nine films were borrowed from the library for use by patrons. The number of inter-
library loan requests filled was 142.
This year the library began to leave a collection of books at the Pearl Brook Housing Center. These books are
changed periodically and if residents there wish any particular books they are welcome to call and request same from
the library. There is also a collection of books at the Annex for the use of the third graders who use that building.
Seven third grade classes come over each week to use the library since there is no library at the Annex.
This year $1,094.06 was collected in fines and turned over to the town. A total of $271 in copying machine
fees and the sum of $45.30 from the sale of old books were turned over to the town. Several new magazines were
ordered this year and they include: The New York Review of Books, Horticulture, Hot Rod, Film Quarterly, Film
Culture, The New Statesman, Ramparts and Horizon.
Books received by the library as gifts came from the following people: Mr. and Mrs. Stephen T. Woodbury,
Mrs. John Loiselle, Mrs. Roger W. Foster, Mr. Richard S. Storrs, Mr. Walter C. White, Mrs. Bigelow Crocker, Jr.,
Mrs. Norman C. Cross, Mr. John R. Proctor, Mr. Donald H. Cragin, Mr. Raymond W. Little, Mrs. Eda Drexler,
Mr. David Foster, Mr. Harold D. Harley, Mrs. Barbara Brown, Mrs. Stuart S. Fay, Richard and Rosemary Cross,
Mrs. William Antoniac and Mr. Harry Dole.
The following people gave books in memory of someone: Mrs. George Upton's Book Club in memory of Mr.
Upton, The Memorial Fund in memory of Isabella Lee Jackson, Mr. & Mrs. Albert Moisan, Sr. in memory of Susan
Moisan and the following people in memory of Ava L. Kelley: Rebecca, Kevin, Lisa and Shawn Kelley, Lena Chase,
Henry and Isabelle Kelley and Pauline Bochner.
The library is open from 10:00A. M. to 9:00P. M. Monday through Thursday, 10:00 A.M. to 5:00 P.M. Fridays,
and 1:00 P.M. to 5:00 P.M. on Saturdays. It is closed on Saturdays during July and August through Labor Day.

Personnel Committee
"Phase 2"
Personnel Committee: Standing left to right: Clarence E. May, Jr. and William 1. Clapp.
Seated left to right: Rudolph P. LeBlanc, Chairman Robert H. Walker and Robert D. Clarke,
We have emerged from the wage-price freeze and now find ourselves in a period called "PHASE II" a time of
restrictive wage and price increases. While there may be many thoughts on how the inflationary spiral should be
checked, it is doubtless commonly accepted that inflation must be controlled. There is nothing to be gained by
carrying home a fatter pay check only to find prices have risen to the point where the added money buys no more,
or perhaps, even less.
Pay scales and fringe benefits of surrounding communities and other towns of Lunenburg's size have been brought
up to date and have been a helpful guide in revising Lunenburg's salary schedule.
The Committee has again met with the various Commissions and Department Heads and is pleased to advise that
the freeze and Phase II restrictions have been met with understanding and cooperation. Requested changes in the
Salary Administration Plan are minor and the recommended wage schedule is within Phase Il guide lines.
5)
In November, Mr. Rudolph P. LeBlanc was appointed to the Personnel Committee to fill the vacancy created
by the resignation of Mr. John E. Rafferty.

Board of Health
The Town of Lunenburg, as a member of the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, receives from that organi-
zation certain professional health services in order that the requirements of the State Sanitary Code and the Regu-
lations of the Board of Health are met. The following is a resume of the health services received during 1971.
Environmental Sanitation
Inspections of Food Handling Establishments
42
Investigations of Nuisance Complaints
83
Inspections and Testing of Private Water Supplies
83
Visits to School Buildings
4
Inspections and Testing of Public Bathing Places
25
Housing Problems Investigated
2
Sewerage Disposal Permits Issued (Repair & New)
66
Inspection/Issuance License for Day Care Centers 3
Consultations, Visits & Inspections of Private Sewerage Disposal Systems
252
Consultations with Board of Health Members and State Officials
20
Well-Child Conferences - These conferences provide health supervision, including immunizations and health tests,
for children who might otherwise not receive this care. Nine Well-Child Clinics were held in Lunenburg attended
by 73 children and a total of 135 immunizations given.
Dental Health - - The dental health program consisted of inspection of 146 students in the first grade and 432 in the
second, fifth and eighth grades. The latter group received inspection, cleaning and a fluoride application. Of all
those examined 44% were found to have significant defects and were referred to their family dentists. Dental health
education was given to all first and fourth graders. All first, second and fifth graders seen by the hygienists were
given complimentary toothbrushes with personal instruction on proper use.
Tine-Testing Program - Early detection through tine testing is still the accepted method of tuberculin skin testing.
A total of 354 tests were done for students in the first and ninth grades.
Rabies Clinic - All sixteen member towns of the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health participated in the Rabies
Clinics and a total of 2,531 dogs were inoculated of which 295 were in Lunenburg.
Council on Aging
"Constitution & By-Laws"
During 1971, meetings of the Council on Aging were held bi-weekly at 131 White Street. Area meetings were
held with the Central Massachusetts Association of Councils on Aging--one in Hudson, Massachusetts and one in
Sterling, Massachusetts. As a result of these area meetings, the Constitution and By-laws of the Councilon Aging
for the year 1971 were drawn up.
Local Council members aided in the election of area officers. The goal of the area organization for the coming
year is to strengthen the local Councils on Aging and unify their activities, as well as to seek available funds from
the State and Federal Governments. Plans are being made for a "get acquainted meeting" in the near future with
the elderly residents of Lunenburg.
The Council on Aging consists of Chairman John M. O'Brien, Arthur J. Brockelman, Sr., Mark G. Thatcher, Sr.,
Philip McNally, Signe. Schultz, Kathlyn M. Cooper, Susie A. Logan, Robert H. Cunningham and Eugene J. Parisen.

Dog Officer
"Sirius" " The Dog Star
If you were to look up intospace you might
catch a glimpse of a brilliant, glittering star,
otherwise known as SIRIUS, the Dog Star.
A subject not likely to be discussed at
the dinner table or at a family gathering, but yet
still there and taken much for granted, is our canine
population still roaming restlessly throughout the
town just like SIRIUS the Dog Star, only to be no-
ticed when sickness or trouble brings him or her
to the limelight still a PAL or PEST-- whatever
one's attitude might reflect.
On numerous occasions during thispast year
dog owners have called this office for assistance in
finding their dogs who had strayed fromhome. Our
greatest problemhas been the countless attacks of
stray dogsupon cattle, horses, sheep and fowl. It
becomes a very difficult task to try and locate these
otherwise friendly household pets once they kill or
maim these unsuspecting animals.
During the past year approximately 21 dogs,
1 horse and 10 to 15 cats were killed by automo-
biles. The total number of calls received in this
office for 1971 was 603 and all showed the deep
concern pet owners have for their animals from
dogs, cats, horses to ducks. A great effort must
still be given to the licensing of younger dogs as
they are most likely to stray.
Dog Officer, August N. Misner.
During the early morning hours our school
yards are still plagued with dogs pulling at the clothing of the school children and darting in front of school buses.
Individual neighborhood grievances relative to complaints on dogs and cats were not of great consequence in 1971
as most problems were resolved by the parties involved, thereby not requiring hearings before the Board of Selectmen.

Board of Appeals
During 1971, the Board of Appeals held public hearings, and acted upon the petitions as follows:
Petition No. 101 - Filed February 8, 1971 by Michael W. Albano, representing Lunenburg Arms Trust, Nash Man-
agement Associates, Inc. and Thomas Baxendale.
Petitions for 1. Reversal of the decision of the Board of Selectmen refusing a permit to construct garden-type
apartments; 2. Approval of apartment-type housing, with reference to Chapter 40B, Section 21 and Chapter
40A, Section 14, of the General Laws; 3. A Variance in order to construct apartment-type housing; and
4. Special Authorization to construct garden-type apartments; on property comprising 50 acres bounded by
Massachusetts Avenue and in part by White Street in Lunenburg, and formerly known as the Levin Farm.
DENIED - April 12, 1971.
In compliance with a Court Order, the Board held a hearing on July 6, 1971, to reconsider the petition to
construct 600 units of low and moderate-income apartment-type housing under the provisions of Chapter 40B,
Sections 20 through 23 of the General Laws of the State of Massachusetts, on the premises designated above.
DENIED - August 13, 1971.
Petitioner appealed this decision with the Housing Appeals Committee in the Department of Community Affairs.
After a hearing, the Housing Appeals Committee determined that the decision of the Board of Appeals was
consistent with local needs, and therefore upheld such decision.
Petition No. 102 - Filed February 22, 1971 by Justin DeFazio.
Petition for Special Authorization to operate a Recreational Family Camping Area consisting of fifty (50) sites
on property located on the easterly side of Reservoir Road in Lunenburg.
DENIED April 12, 1971.
Petition No. 103 - Filed April 14, 1971 by Vieno M. Kingston.
Petition for a Variance in order to use a second building on premises owned and occupied by him at West Townsend
Road in Lunenburg, for dwelling purposes.
GRANTED WITH RESTRICTION - June 9, 1971.
Petition No. 104 - Filed May 20, 1971 by Kenneth B. Saucier.
Petition for Special Authorization to operate, on a part-time basis, a boat livery, including a bait shop, at
261 Prospect Street in Lunenburg.
GRANTED WITH RESTRICTION June 13, 1971.
Petition No. 105 - Filed May 17, 1971 by Vito J. Cucchiara.
Petition for Special Authorization to operate an open-air sales and display area for no more than five (5) travel
trailers, on an existing commercial lot at the corner of Massachusetts Avenue and Chase Road in Lunenburg.
GRANTED - June 24, 1971.
Petition No. 106 - Filed June 1, 1971 by Cross Country Corporation.
Petition for Special Authorization for open-air sales and demonstrations of camper trailers, all-terrain vehicles,
snowmobiles and mini-bikes on commercially zoned property on the southerly side of Massachusetts Avenue,
near 228 Massachusetts Avenue in Lunenburg.
DENIED WITHOUT PREJUDICE - August 13, 1971.
Petition No. 107 - Filed June 1, 1971 by Leonard J. LeBlanc.
Petition fora Variance in order to construct an addition to adwelling less than the required distance from one
side line of premises located on Sunset Lane in Lunenburg.
DENIED - July 20, 1971.

Building Committee
"First Mission Accomplished"
During the past year, various assignments have been undertaken by the Building Committee, one of which is the
completion of the new highway facility.
Estimates for a salt bin at the new highway facility and repairs to the main building at the old highway garage
on West Street, which is presently utilized by the Park Commission, were obtained for the Board of Selectmen.
A study of space requirements for the Police and Fire Departments was initiated. Preliminary results indicate that
a modest addition to the existing Fire Station would satisfy Fire Department requirements for the next 15 to 20 years.
On the other hand, Police Department needs are very acute and more intense study into this complex need is required.
Present police facilities do not contribute to effective functioning of that department.
The resignations of Mr. Philip E. Pearson and Mr. Charles H. Hillman were accepted with regret. Both gentlemen
served on the Building Committee since its inception. Mr. Roger A. Edman was appointed to fill the vacancy created
by the resignation of Mr. Hillman and the remaining vacancy is to be filled shortly after the Annual Town Meeting in
March of 1972.
WIRM
THREE
Building Committee: Left to right - Chairman Camille J. LeBlanc, Jr., Robert N. Fickett, John G. Davis
and Elbridge G. Hoag. Absent when picture was taken were Roger A. Edman and Francis A. Harding.

Conservation Commission & Town Forest Committee
"Watch Dogs"
People of Lunenburg wake up and livel It may be later than you think. Your time may be running out.
The countdown mayhave already started. The fateful day may come when you and your children and your children's
children could wake up (if they wake up at all) without air to breathe, water to drink, or food to eat. Our natural
resources could be destroyed, our ecology wrecked and there may be no environment. We can pour millions of dol-
lars into educating our children to live in a world that in twenty years may find water unfit to drink and the oxygen
level too low for human survival.
Today's challenge is to find ways of living in our environment without destroying it. And to this end, your
committees have worked, acting as "watchdogs", protecting the wetlands for the recharging of our water supply,
acquiring areas of forest which not only produce oxygen and encourage wildlife, but also provide areas of undevel-
oped land for the recharging of the human spirit. Our aim is still to set aside at least 4,000 acres for conservation
purposes.
We are particularly grateful to the Lunenburg Memorial Fund for their part in making possible the purchase of
six picnic tables for use in the Ben Normand area, Cowdrey Nature Center and Clark's Hill Reservation. Their gift
also included an aluminum rowboat for use in the Mulpus Swamp, as well as the planting of forty-seven shrubs and
trees, to provide both food and shelter for animals, in the Clark's Hill Reservation.
"Help save our town --- Keep it beautiful and a nice place to live in. =
Conservation Commission and Town Forest Committee: Standing - Left to right - Dorothy Millington, Alfred C.
Gilchrest and Richard A. Brockelman. Seated - Left to right - Mrs. Reginald Peabody, Harold D. Harley,
Chairman Nancy Sholl and Willis C. Woodruff. Absent when picture was taken were: Ralph S. Foster Sr. and
Richard C. Hayes.

School Housing Study Committee
Leveling Off In Progress" "
After having met several times, including a meeting with Superintendent of Schools, Dr. William R. Holland,
we present an optimistic report as compiled by Dr. Holland andby The Arthur D. Little Company. It shouldbe men-
tioned that The Arthur D. Little Company conducted a survey, free of charge to Lunenburg, using a new enrollment
forecasting system. While both Dr. Holland and The Arthur D. Little Company differed in extent, both came to the
conclusion that the school population has leveled off and appears to be decreasing.
Without making specific recommendations, it appears that with this trend the Town should concentrate on bring-
ing the present school plants up-to-date where required. This is described more in detail in the Superintendent's
report. The Town, while seemingly n good shape with its school housing, now should be prepared for a possible change
in this status with the current trend towards large scale residential development. Shown below is an updated school
enrollment graph--1960-1976.
The School Housing Study Committee consists of Chairman Charles J. Darling, Herbert N. Hoffman, Howard
F. Brock, Radcliffe P. Cheesman, Richard P. Bickford, Ralph B. Goguen and Charles H. Hillman.

Development & Industrial Commission
"Matters Of Factual Consideration"
The Development & Industrial Commission's activity during the past year was rather limited in scope, undoubtedly
affected by the poor economic conditions that prevailed in the area as evidenced by the business cut-backs in the
neighboring cities and the high unemployment levels.
When competing for new business, especially during bad times, one must realize how muchmore handicapped
the Town of Lunenburg is in relationship to the surrounding area. For example, we do not have an adequate water supply
or sewerage to support industry. Our only plentiful resource is land but even here we are disadvantaged since our
Town Protective By-laws do not include industrial zoning. In years past many inquiries were made concerning
plant sites, but when confronted with no industrial zoning, these companies located elsewhere.
This past year we did have an opportunity to improve our positic regarding water supply. However, the voters
of the Water District saw fit to vote down a proposal to erect a much needed standpipe. The Development & Industrial
Commission, working through the Overall Economic Development Program Committee for the Fitchburg-Leominster
Area, has made a request for federal funds so that water might be provided to the commercially zoned property on
Chase Road and on Leominster-Shirley Road.
Under the Public Works and Economic Development Act of 1965, an areamay request government help when an
area's unemployment level reaches a critical stage. Although the request is based upon future industrial use, obviously
the Town and individual homeowners would benefit should the water system be expanded. The Lunenburg Water District
Commissioners have assisted with and lent their support to this request.
The members of the Development & Industrial Commission feel some light industry is desirable and should be
included in the normal growth of the Town. The argument used to discourage industrial growth is that new business
creates new jobs, and new jobs mean new homes and families, and new homes and families mean increased school
population, which in turn means more taxes for everyone since the tax money gained from real estate may not be enough
to cover the increases to the school budget. This concept is basically sound, and industry if not properly planned for
could indeed adversely affect the tax rate. However, one fact remains crystal clear--if Lunenburg remains as it is,
the most desirable bedroom community in the area, then as our neighboring cities and towns develop their industry
we will acquire a disproportionate share of new homes and families without receiving a single tax dollar from
industry.
The Montachusett Regional Planning Commission in a recent report has made the following population projections
for the next ten years: Leominster up 17.3%, Lunenburg up 16.8%, Townsend up 16.5%, Westminster up 6.1%, and
Fitchburg and Shirley to drop slightly. This increase in Lunenburg represents 1,247 people and is slightly greater
than the 1,085 gain between 1960 and 1970. Already we can see a major housing development being planned for the
Pleasant Street area in the immediate future.
Other arguments focus on ecology. Some individuals feel industry is a noisy polluter and an eyesore If prop-
erly planned for and with today's rigid anti-pollution laws this is not the case. One need only comparea recently
built plant to those constructed several years ago to see the dramatic differences in layout and landscaping.
Industry per se is not a godsend. It cannot and will not significantly reduce taxes, but if properly planned
for, it can be a tremendous stabilizer and can become a decelerator for ever increasing taxes.
- 22
===PAGE 25===
For example, if one of our larger tracts of commercially zoned property were zoned industrial this in itself
should discourage construction of new homes in and around the area. Also, if planned properly, other large tracts of
land surrounding the property could be put into conservation so as to create a buffer between adjoining residential
areas and prevent the spread of industry into these more populated areas. Then, if water were tobe provided to this
site in sufficientquantity to just handle fire protection and limited process usage, we could have a fairly attractive
piece of real estate that could undoubtedly be developed as an industrial park for light industry. Through planning
and control of size, location and services, industrial development of this type can provide some form of tax relief.
The Development & Industrial Commission will put before the votersduring Town Meeting anarticle that is not
binding and asks for opinion regarding future industrial growth. The purpose of this article is strictly for guidance
not only to this Commission but to all elected and appointed officials of the Town of Lunenburg.
Should the thought of industrial growth, even though it maybe limited in scope, be repugnant to the voters of
Lunenburg, they must keep in mind the dilemma that faces them in the future. Taxes will continue to rise along with
new home construction. Ultimately we will be faced with the need for additional services that must accompany this
growth such as water, sewerage, fire and police protection, schools, roads and rubbish disposal, to name a few. If
industry is not to be included in this growth then alternate approaches must be found now if we are ever to control
the tax spiral.
Development and Industrial Commission: left to right - Timothy S. Hillman, Ronald Wilson,
Norman C. Cross, Chairman Frederick W. Stenuis, Bigelow Crocker, Jr. and Mendall W.
Colburn. Absent when picture was taken was Rodney A. Karlson.

===PAGE 26===
Town Government Study Committee
"Study Progressing"
Town Government Study Committee: left to right - Cynthia G. Walker,
Chairman George B. Larkin, Philip M. Crouse and Wilbur W. Barney.
During 1971 we conducted interviews with elected and appointed members of various Committees and Boards.
We discussed with them the operation of town government as viewed from their present positions with special emphasis
on their opinions of the strengths and weaknesses of the government as it presently exists in their own and other areas.
We also had the opportunity to read many articles and reference material concerning town government in general.
As a result of our studies and discussions, we submit the following recommendations:
1. Zoning By-Laws - The three areas of town business which are most time-consuming for the Selectmen are:
the Highway Department, Board of Health and administration of the zoning by-laws. We recommend that an
enforcement officer be appointed by the Selectmen to administer the zoning by-laws. He should be specifically
charged with reviewing and checking on all applications and permits issued for new buildings or alterations,
to make sure that the plans presented under the by-laws are adhered to. The Board of Assessors, the Board of
Health and the Planning Board must be kept appraised of all developments. Adequate remuneration for the time
involved should be offered. By relieving the Selectmen of this duty, we would expect stricter supervision over
the departments under their control. We would recommend that they spend more time in long-range plans for
the direction of town growth.
- 24
===PAGE 27===
2. Secretary to the Board of Selectmen- The addition of a full time Secretary to the Board of Selectmen has
certainly resulted in a more orderly transaction of town business, as well as much saving of time for the Select-
men as individuals. We recommend that a Central Purchasing Division for all town departments be set upunder
his control for more economical and better supervised ordering and distribution of all necessary supplies. A
more thorough knowledge of all available State and Federal funds which might help the economy of the Town
should be acquired under his direction.
3. Town Meeting and Election- We recommend that the Annual Town Meeting and the Annual Town Election
be held on two separate days; the Annual Town Election to be held on the first Saturday in March between
the hours of 8:00 A.M. and 5:00 P.M., at which time the Town Report and Warrant would be available to all
voters, and the Annual Town Meeting to be held on the second Saturday in March at 1:00 P.M.
4. Building Committee We recommend that the duties of the Building Committee be altered to include the
direction and planning of needed town buildings. The Committee should be charged with the responsibility
of evaluating the existing facilities for all Town Departments (Police, Fire, Park, Town offices, etc.) and of
making long-range plans for filling future needs.
These recommendations have reference only to the most obvious problems pointed out during our study. It is
the feeling of this Committee that we have only made a start on the total evaluation of the desirability of changing
the town government structure. The consideration of Town Manager, Executive Secretary and/or Representative Town
Meeting warrant further study. We, therefore, request that this Committee be continued for another year to complete
its investigations and that the present vacancy on this Committee created by the resignation of William G. Landis
be filled after the Annual Town Meeting. We wish to thank all the people who shared their time and their thoughts
with US during the course of this study. Their help and suggestions are much appreciated.
Refuse Disposal Planning Committee
"MRDA" Study Underway
The ban on open fires which became effective last year created a problem for Lunenburg as well as for most.
other towns in the Commonwealth. Our Municipal Refuse Disposal Area is destined to run out of space at an accel-
erating rate thus creating a potential health hazard. This Committee was, therefore, appointed in accordance
with Article 11 of the Annual Town Meeting on March 6, 1971 to solve this problem.
The Committee met a total of fourteen times during the year, seven of which were with representatives of the
Towns of Ashby, Shirley and Townsend. Our efforts to establish aregional district or at least to engage a consultant
to conduct a regional feasibility study proved fruitless. This was mostly because the Townsend group could not agree
on the same consultant for the Town Study and the Regional Study, and the Shirley group felt that they could solve
their problem in cooperation with the State facility at the Industrial School.
We engaged an engineering company, Sanitas Associates, to study our problem. Their preliminary report indi-
cates that our present Municipal Refuse Disposal Area site can be converted into a sanitary landfill and will last for
about twenty years. The final report is due shortly and will include recommendations for future action. The Committee
has committed $2,300 of its $3,500 appropriation and feels confident that it can complete its study within the limits
of this appropriation. We recommend that the Committee be continued for one more year.
The Refuse Disposal Planning Committee consists of: Chairman Sherwood G. Winchester, Ralph M. Bickford
Jr., and Seymour L. Schnuer.

Highway Department
Emphasis on Anti-Pollution Measures"
When one considers the investment that the American public has in highway facil-
ities and motor vehicles, obviously, it is imperative that they be of use to them during
any time of the year regardless of the weather. We cannot deny the motorist the use of
his vehicle or of the highway for even short periods of time because they are covered
with snow and ice.
To keep the roadways clear, we have placed a great deal of dependency on chem-
icals - both sodium chloride and calcium chloride. The use and demand of chemicals
has continued to grow year after year, and with environmental pollution being uppermost
in the minds of many Americans today, much criticism has been directed to the winter
maintenance men.
Because of this emphasis on anti-pollution measures, a report was prepared by the
Highway Research Board which defines the scope of the highway pollution problemre-
sulting from the use of highway road salts. This report states that not all salt in the en-
Richard W. Brown
vironment comes from that being applied to roads in the winter time. Approximately 35
Highway Superintendent
to 40 pounds of salt per acre fall in normal rainfall each year. Strong winds from the
sea also pickup and deposit much chloride on the continental land mass near the oceans.
Other sources of chlorides which may cause pollution are surface runoff, industrial wastes and sewerage plants.
Chlorides in the environment are measured in parts per million (ppm). To get some idea of the relative size of one
part per million, one must think in terms of one inch in sixteen miles. Water containing as many as 2,000 ppm has
been used for human consumption without adverse effects on the human body.
There are several areas where highway road salts have been accused of creating pollution problems. Firstly,
fish have been killed in farm ponds and small lakes immediately adjacent to highways where salt has been used on
the highway. This is a legitimate problem which results from extremely high concentrations entering these bodies
without sufficient water from other sources to dilute the salt. It is claimed that deep wells supplying water for do-
mestic consumption have been polluted from highway road salts, even though the wells were located several hundred
yards from the highway. This claim is difficult to disprove and is usually untrue unless water-bearing shales or sand
lead directly from the roadway to the wells.
Most roadside vegetation can withstand concentrations of the chlorides up to 1, 200 ppm and a grass with a good
salt tolerance can resist chloride levels of up to 4,500 ppm. Some trees and bushes are sensitive to the presence of
even less than 2,000 ppmof salt, such as maples, certain poplars, larch, roses, multiflora rose and barberry. Trees
with a good tolerance for salt are the oaks, locusts and hawthornes, and will usually withstand up to 6,000 ppm of
salt in soil around them. The pollutant factor is not simply the amount of chloride present, but the amount of chem-
ical related to the amount of water present.
While there are several alternatives to the uses of chlorides on roads, none of them appear at this time to be
feasible, and we will have to depend upon the melting action of these chemicals for some time to come. The only
alternative to discontinuing the use of chlorides as de-icing agents would be to discontinue bare pavement maintenance
and allow snow to build up on the streets between each storm. However, the American public would not tolerate
a return to the conditions that prevailed 20 years ago. (Information in this report was adapted from paper present-
ed by R.N. Hughmanick at the 11th Annual North American Snow Conference.)
The following is an explanation of services rendered and projects accomplished by the Highway Deparment on
the Town's 76.97 miles of accepted roads:
Chapter 81. The following roads were treated with approximately 12,080 gallons of asphalt with 510 yards of sand
for cover: Page, Elmwood, Oak, Kimball, N.W. Townsend, Pleasant, Lesure, Pierce, Elizabeth, Wallis, Florence,
Clearing, Kirby, Tilton, Pleasant View, School, Joslin, Burke, Natick, Hamlin, Otis, Piedmont and Skylark Lane.
Approximately 673 tons of Bituminous Concrete was laid in place on Prospect Street, Whalom Road and Summer Street.
A section of Beal Street was widened, and a section of Elmwood Road was gravelled with 583 yards utilized. Ap-
proximately 272 tons of patch was used to fill holes in various roads. Catch basin cleaning, spring cleaning, brush
and shoulder cutting and roadside mowing were also accomplished under this account.
- 26
===PAGE 29===
General Highway Maintenance. Brown Drive was excavated, widened and drainage installed. Drainage was also
installed in asection of West Street. Approximately 318 tons of patch was used on various roads and 69 tons of stone
dust was utilized to fill in frost heaves. An extensive dip was filled in on Page Street, using 156 yards of gravel for
this purpose. Roadside mowing, spring cleaning, application of calcium chloride, purchase of construction signs
and small tools also came under this account.
Chapter 90 Maintenance. Approximately 1,606 tons of bituminous concrete was laid in place on Whalom Road, Sum-
mer Street and Electric Avenue.

tance of approximately 4,000 feet was reconstructed, widened, drainage installed and slopes graded at a cost of
$52,351.96. Another section of this road will be reconstructed in 1972.
Road Oil & Tar. The following streets were treated with 27,898 gallons of asphalt and 1,451 yards of sand for cover
at a cost of $11,000: Kimball, Page, Skylark, Ramgren, Elmwood, Hollis, West, Graham, Beal, Leominster, Whiting,
Turkey Hill, South Row, Gilchrest, Pond, Sunnyhill, Savage and Brown Drives.
Snow Removal. The purchase of the 1971 Trojan loader was aboon in removing piles of snow at road intersections.
The cost of snow removal was $69,881.95.
Road Machinery Maintenance. The hub of the whole Highway Department operation lies in this category. Real-
izing that breakdown of equipment is not only costly, but seriously affects the extent and efficiency of work performed,
our mechanics endeavor to spot repairs while they are still minor in a conscientious effort to save the town money.
Under this account, gas, oil, grease, acetylene and oxygen, tires, chains, plow and graderblades are purchased,
as well as all materials relative to repairs.
Special Accounts. in continuance of our drainage program, 502 feet of pipe was installed in Sunnyhill Road, 920
feet in Kimball Street, 1, 409 feet in Ramgren Road and 439 feet in Lancaster Avenue, along with 1,351 yards of gravel
and 292 tons of trap at a cost of $15,800.00. Pine Grove Road and Lakeview Avenue were reconstructed and widened
at a cost of $10,000.00.

Police Department
"Caught In The Act"
Statistics cannot be compiled as to how many
crimes have been prevented as result of the advent
of 24hour coverage. Easier to document are the ar-
rests made in at least ten different breaks or larcenies.
Persons were "caught in the act" or apprehended after
breaks into homes, businesses and thefts of personal
property--all because there was an officer on night
duty. The value of merchandise recovered far offsets
the expense incurred by the Town in providing for the
XXXX
additional coverage.
In 1971, with the indoctrination of 24hour cov-
erage and the resignation of two officers, the reserve
force of the Police Department was put to a true test
and all who had participated in the voluntary training
program were utilized to fill the gaps. They did a
commendable job and helped provide coverage during
a period that could have posed a serious threat to our
community.
With the addition of two patrolmen under the
provisions of the Emergency Employment Act and with
plans to replace the sixth regular patrolman lost by
resignation, the Police Department will have a com-
Chief of Police, Frederick B. Hobbs.
plement of eight full-time men. Thiswi II give Lunen-
burg sufficient manpower for basic 24 hour coverage
which is comparable to other communities in our population range.
In December, Patrolman August N. Misner began an eight week police training course which is required formal
training for all regular officers. The course is conducted in conjunction with the Worcester Police Department under
the supervision of the Massachusetts Police Training Council and is paid for by the Town. Additionally, there are
a number of regular and reserve officers who are enrolled in law enforcement courses through Northeastern University
at their own time and expense.
For the first time since the all-night parking ban was enacted in 1953, the addition of a night duty officer has
enabled the enforcement of this by-law. This perhaps has not been very popular with some residents, as under the new
"no-warning" system a $5.00 fine results for the first offense. However, persons who feel they have been illegally
tagged for this offense have the option of requesting a court hearing.
Flea Markets, Barn Sales and similar activities were increasingly popular in 1971 and some posed a question
in regard to Sunday Blue Laws. These laws provide that only the Chief of Police in a community may grant permis-
sion to conduct a business on Sunday, with a specification of uses for which a permit may be granted. A closer
check will be made in 1972 of operations without a Sunday permit and appropriate action will be taken against those
in violation.
- 28
===PAGE 31===
During the past year, new office equipment, badly needed firearms and related items, and additional finger-
printing and crime detection devices were purchased. Protective screening was installed in the cruisers and has
proven to be most valuable for the protection and safety of officers.
Breaks into homes and businesses continued and many burglar alarms have been installed. Of interest is the
fact that of all the alarms that have been tied into the communications center, only one was actually tripped in the
commission of a crime, and in this instance those responsible were apprehended in the building. Residents considering
an alarm system are advised to contact several companies to compare and investigate the worth of the system before
purchase.
The program for upgrading and replacing trafficsigns appears to having some effect. This budget is limited
and requests for signs must conform with requirements stipulated by the Massachusetts Department of Public Works.
Being recommended this year is the procurement of a third cruiser, additional communication equipment and
a paint lining machine, all of which have been included in articles for the 1972 Annual Town Meeting Warrant.
Lunenburg has the reputation of having a fine Police Department and this is due to the support of Town officials
and the responsible and dedicated officers within the department. Gratitude is expressed for the cooperation and
courtesy extended by the Townspeople which more than contributes to the overall effectiveness of this department.

Fire Department
"Filled To Capacity"
In 1971 your Fire Department answered 242 calls for assistance as follows:
Rescue Calls 89; Brush Fires 25; Service Calls 25; Car Fires 22; Disposal Area 21;
Building Fires 16; Mutual Aid 11; Accidents 10; Accidental Alarms 6; Electrical
Fires 4; Needless Calls 3; Chimney Fires 2; Cooking Fires 3; Lawnmower Fires 2;
False Alarms 1; Dog Rescue 1; Mattress Fire 1. Of these calls, 92 were alarms. The
largest increase was in Rescue Unit Calls, going from 55 calls in 1970 to 89 calls
in 1971. Other increases were in car fires, disposal area calls and mutual aid calls
and a decrease was noted in brush fire calls. The burningban as of July 1st of 1971
and the average rainfall accounted for this decrease.
In the three years the Rescue Unit has been in operation, we have gone from
56 calls in 1969, 55 calls in 1970 to 89 calls in 1971. In 1971 we transported 85
patients. This large increase is due, in part, to the fact that more residents are now
aware that this service is available in Lunenburg, thus more calls are received. With
new dispatchers and additional police officers our Unit has been called when in the
Fire Chief James P. Deming
past other means would have been used to transport patients. We have also received
more calls for respiratory problems than in the past. We are still operating on the
same basis--two squads with seven men on each squad, alternating on calls. The Unit is under an annual inspection
by the State Department of Public Health. All squad members are registered with the State Department of Public
Health and hold advanced Red Cross Cards and attend an advanced Red Cross refresher course annually.
Bi-weekly meetings and monthly fire drills were held with excellent attendance. Six firefighters attended a
two-day Fire School held in Fitzwilliam, New Hampshire. One more dry hydrantwas installed this year off Good-
rich Street at the William Brown farm and additional hydrants are planned for 1972.
Our most pressing need is an addition to the Fire Station. The garage area hasbeen filled to capacity for three
years. There is not sufficient space for a work area, storage area for firehose and miscellaneous equipment, or wash
area. One truck should be replaced in the next few years but there is not enough floor space to accommodate a re-
placement. Our fire alarmsystem should be updated to air horns but there is no room for the related equipment. In
case of a power failure we would have no alarm system. Reasonable expansion and growth of the Fire Department
and its facilities is necessary to keep pace with the growth of the Town.

Veterans' Services
"Procedure Defined"
The year 1971 has been a year of unemployment, freezes and increased case loads for this department. As the
veteran becomes more and more in need of assistance, he turns to this department for aid. The following is the case
load and monthly average case load from 1969 through 1971:
1969
1970
1971
Case Loads As Reported to the State:
143
181
217
Average (monthly):
12
15
18
Expended:
$24,471.00
$33,623.00
$54,000.00

$12,235.50
$16,811.50
$27,000.00
In 1971 the State Commissioner deemed it necessary to allow a 3.8% cost of living (COL) increase across the
board on all budgets and has in his office approval for a 5% COL increase for the early part of 1972. This procedure
is outlined as follows: a. Each applicant is in receipt of a certain amount of income--Social Security, VA Pension,
Workmen's Compensation, Insurance, Unemployment Compensation. b. Abudget is worked out for the veteran and/or
his dependents. This budget never varies unless increased by the Commissioner. C. The amount of income, regardless
of source, is subtracted from the overall budget with the recipient receiving the remainder. However, if his income
is in excess of the budget he is declared "ineligible". d. Upon approval of his application, and of the amounts to
be expended, the veteran is notified by letter of his eligibility. This eligibility includes medical, dental and other
monthly accrued benefits.
Pursuant to the directives received in 1971, no case can be designated as permanent or temporary. Each case
is given a Veterans' Service Number which is to be used on all applications wherever and whenever the veteran makes
known he is in need of assistance. When a veteran is
receiving assistance from a town and moves to another
town, he can make application at his new place of res-
idency. Upon request, all documents relative to the
veteran's case are forwarded to the Agent at his new
place of residency and the veteran will then continue
to receive assistance.
Further information regarding residency require-
ments in the Commonwealth of Massachusetts is that a
veteran making application for assistance must reside in
that town for at least one day if he entered his military
service from Massachusetts. If not, he must reside in
the Commonwealth at least three years.
This department is amazed at the number of vet-
erans who are unaware of benefits available to them and
even unaware of this department. Chapter 115 of the
General Laws, which governs this department, states
that every city, town and hamlet will have a Veterans'
Agent and that three to six towns may be serviced by
one Agent without being considered a district.
Regional of this department is foreseen in
the near future as we are now under the Secretary of
Human Resources and as our counter-partner Welfare
has been regionalized, so then, possibly, will we.
Veteran's Agent, Alfred H. Gauthier.

Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fitting
The Inspector of Plumbing and Gas Fitting is legally desig-
nated by law with the responsibility of administering and enforc-
ing the provisions of the Massachusetts State Plumbing Code.
He is charged with interpreting the requirements of the
Massachusetts State Plumbing Code and with the granting of vari-
ances which in his opinion are justifiable. Basic and sanitary prin-
ciples of plumbing construction are embodied in the Plumbing
Code. It's primary object is the conservation of public health
from the deleterious effects which experience has shown arise from
unsanitary and insufficient plumbing work due to the lack of tech-
nical knowledge and skill of those performing the work.
The construction of prefabricated homes and/or modular
homes in Lunenburg must comply with the Massachusetts State
Plumbing Code and must be inspected in the manner prescribed in
said Code before occupancy is permitted.
During 1971 there were a total of 166 Plumbing and Gas
Permits issued. Sixty-three plumbing permits for remodeled
Inspector of Plumbing and Gas Fitting
homes, 18 for new homesand 5 for commercial buildings. Five gas
Thomas E. Mortis
permits were issued for commercial buildings, 18 for new homes
and 57 for a variety of gas appliances.
Inspector of Weights & Measures
In accordance with provisions of Section 41, Chapter 98 of
the Massachusetts General Laws, all inhabitants or persons having
usual places of business within the Town using weighing devices
for selling goods, wares, or merchandise, for public weighing, or
for hire or reward, are hereby notified tobring them in to be re-
tested, adjusted and sealed. The scales utilized by this depart-
ment range from one gram to three hundred thousand pounds.
Scales, gasoline pumps, fuel oil truck meters and other
commercially used weighing and measuring devices are regularly
tested for accuracy once a year and unannounced spot checks are
frequently made. Of the 130 weighing and measuring devices
inspected in 1971, 126 were found to be correct andsealed. Two
pumps and two scales were condemned.
Lunenburg residents have the right to expect that whatever
they buy is measured or weighed accurately--to assure this ac-
curacy is the responsibility of the Inspector of Weights and
Measures.
The Inspector of Weights and Measures extends his appreci-
ation to the various establishments in the Town of Lunenburg for
Inspector of Weights and Measures
the cooperation and assistance rendered.
Wayne A. Balcom

Town Nurse
"A Healthful Hand"
The Town of Lunenburg employs two
full-time registered nurses, Mrs. Doris M.
Lahti and Mrs. Gladys V. Barney. Mrs.
Shirley M. Carpenter replaced Mrs. Jean
Hobbs in September of 1971 as the Town's
part-time nurse. Mrs. Carpenter works five
hours a day when schools are in session.
The above nurses perform visiting nursing
for the Town as well as all nursing functions
for the schools. Mrs. Marjorie Faulkner
works part time doing the clerical work
connected with the Nursing Program.
Through the Nashoba Associated
Boards of Health nine (9) Well-Child Clin-
icswere heldwith Dr. Benjamin Blechman,
Doris M. Lahti
a Pediatrician from Lowell, as attending
Gladys V. Barney
Town Nurse
physician. A total of 73 infants and pre-
Assistant Town Nurse
schoolers made 121 visits for health super-
vision and physical examinations. Also, immunizations were given as needed for the following: Diphtheria, Tetanus
and Pertussis Series and Boosters, Trivalent Oral Polio Series and Boosters, Vaccinations, Tuberculosis Tine Testing,
Measles, Mumps and German Measles. Any mother wishing more information about the Well-Child Clinic may call
582-6702.
The Lunenburg Public Health Nursing Service's contract with "Burbank Coordinated Home Health Care" con-
tinues to provide discharged patients with excellent care when necessary, including nursing visits, services of Home
Health Aides, Dietician, Physio, Occupational and Speech Therapists, and also equipment when needed.
The nursing fee for Bedside Nursing is $6.00 per visit. However, no patient is refused care because of inabil-
ity to pay. During 1971 a total of 982 nursing visits were made, classified as follows:
Bedside Nursing Visits
722
Adult Health Supervision Visits
92
Visits to School Children
112
Pre-School Visits
20
Infant & Premature Infant Visits
15
Pre-natal & Post-natal Visits
21
Of the above visits, 581 were made to patients over 65 years of age for Bedside
Nursing and Health Promotion.
Throughout the year the nurses attended service meetings and lectures at
Nashoba, Rutland Central Health Office, Burbank Hospital and also special
meetings in Boston and Worcester.
We wish to thank the Woman's Club of Lunenburg for providing transpor-
tation to the Well-Child Clinics, and their Health Service Group and the United
Parish Service Group for giving layettes, mittens, hats, and other knitted articles
Shirley M. Carpenter
to distribute to Lunenburg families.
Part-time Nurse

Article 2. To accept the regular annual written reports of the Town Officers and Committees, C. act anything thereon.
VOTED UNANIMOUSLY THAT THE REGULAR ANNUAL WRITTEN REPORTS OF THE TOWN OFFICERS AND COMMITTEES RE-

Article 3. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of
the Town of Lunenburg" by deleting the Classification and Salary Schedule voted on March 7, 1970 and substituting therefor
of Classification and Salary Schedule proposed by the Personnel Board and dated March 6, 1971; by adding after the third sentence
deleting the last three items of pargraph 1 of Section 17 and substituting therefor "After completion of 10 months continuous service
paragraph 1 of Section 16 the following new sentence: "Only time actually worked shall be used in computing overtime"; by
of 10 15 paid days (2 calendar weeks), after completion of 10 years continuous service 15 paid days (3 calendar weeks), after completion
years continuous service 20 paid days (4 calendar weeks)"; by adding the following new paragraph at the end of Section 17
the "The minimum allowable time charged against vacation shall be four (4) hours. "; by striking out Section 18 and substituting therefor
following new section: Section 18. PAID HOLIDAYS. "The Town of Lunenburg shall observe the following holidays: New Year's
Day, Washington's Birthday, Patriot's Day, Memorial Day, Independence Day, Labor Day, Columbus Day, Thanksgiving Day,
- 66 -
===PAGE 69===
Veterans' Day, Christmas Day". PERMANENT FULL TIME EMPLOYEES (40 hours): The employee is entitled toeach of the sched-
uled holidays or the equivalent time off with paybased on the hourly rate of the employee for aneight (8) hour day. PERMANENT
PART TIME EMPLOYEES: The employee who normally works twenty (20) or more hours per week is entitled to each of the listed
holidays or the equivalent time off with pay based upon the number of hours resulting from dividing his normally scheduled hours
in a week by five (5). Holiday time will be given on the holiday if possible, if not, equivalent time off with pay will be granted.
However, if according to the needs of the Department, holiday time or equivalent time off cannot be granted, the employee shall
receive payment at double the hourly base rate in lieu of holiday time. Whenever a holiday falls on Sunday, it will be observed
on the Monday following. Whenever a holiday falls on Saturday, holiday time will be scheduled by the Department Head. To be
eligible for holiday pay, it is necessary that an employee work his full assigned shift on his regularly scheduled work day before
a holiday, and the next regularly scheduled working day following aholiday, unless the employee has been excused for a legitimate
reason by the Department Head. Holiday time will not be cumulative and must be taken within either three (3) weeks before or
three (3) weeks following the specified holiday. Only time actually worked shall be used in computing overtime.; and by adding
the following new paragraph at the end of Section 19: "The minimum allowable time charged against sick leave shall be two hours",
or act anything thereon. (Disapproved by the Finance Committee because the Finance Committee does not believe Lunenburg can
afford the expense of increasing the "Classification and Salary Schedule voted March 7, 1970" and recommends that the Town retain
the existing 1970 Classification and Salary Schedule.) VOTED.
