Article 5. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law of the Town of Lunenburg by adding at the end of
the first paragraph of Section V-7--"For purposes of this paragraph the width of the shall mean the minimum distance measured
through the building and parallel to the street from one side lot to the other side lot line", and by striking out the first sentence
of Section IV-1 and inserting in place thereof the following: "In a Residence A, Residence B and Outlying district, no building
except a detached building of accessory use, shall be constructed on a lot having less area than the "required lot area" or having
less width (than the minimum distance through the building and parallel to the street from one side lot to the other side lot line)
than the required lot width through building" specified in the following table for the district in which said lot is located."
VOTED THAT THE PROTECTIVE BY-LAW OF THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG BE AMENDED BY ADDING AT THE END OF THE
FIRST PARAGRAPH OF SECTION V-7-- "FOR PURPOSES OF THIS PARAGRAPH THE WIDTH OF THE LOT SHALL MEAN THE
MINIMUM DISTANCE MEASURED THROUGH THE BUILDING AND PARALLEL TO THE STREET FROM ONE SIDE LOT TO THE
OTHERS LOT LINE", AND BY STRIKING OUT THE FIRST SENTENCE OF SECTION IV-1 AND INSERTING IN PLACE THERE-
OF THE FOLLOWING: "IN A RESIDENCE A, RESIDENCE B AND OUTLYING DISTRICT, NO BUILDING EXCEPT A DETACHED
BUILDING OF ACCESSORY USE, SHALL BE CONSTRUCTED ON A LOT HAVING LESS AREA THAN THE "REQUIRED LOT AREA"
OR HAVING LESS WIDTH (MEASURED THROUGH THE BUILDING AT THE NARROWEST PART OF THE LOT AND PARALLEL TO
THE STREET FROM ONE SIDE LOT TO THE OTHERSID LOT LINE)THAN THE "REQUIRED LOT WIDTH THROUGH BUILDING"
SPECIFIED IN THE FOLLOWING TABLE FOR THE DISTRICT IN WHICH SAID LOT IS LOCATED. YES - 154 NO - 2
Article 6. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law by adding to Section V-7 at the end of the first
paragraph the following: For purposes of this paragraph, the width of the lot shall mean the minimum distance measured through
the building and parallel to the street from one side lot to the other side lot line; and change the definition of width of a lot in
parenthesis in Section IV-1 to read (than the minimum distance measured through the building and parallel to the street fromone
side lot to the other side lot line), or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO PASS OVER ARTICLE 6.
Article 7. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law by adding after Section V-9, Section V-10 which
will regulate off-street parking in all districts and which will regulate loading areas in commercial districts, or act anything
thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY THAT THE PROTECTIV BY-LAW OF THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG BE AMENDED BY ADDING
AFTER SECTION V-9, A NEW SECTION TO BE KNOWN AS V-10, OFF STREET PARKING AND LOADING AREAS, AS FOLLOWS:
SECTION V-10. Off-Street Parking and Loading Areas.
A. Off-street parking and loading spaces shall be required as follows:
1. Dwellings: Two (2) parking spaces for each dwelling unit therein.
2. Place of assembly, including a funeral home: One (1) parking space for each three (3) seats therein or one (1) space
for each sixty (60) inches of bleachers or benches.
3. Schools: One (1) parking space for each classroom and office therein, plus one (1) parking space for each three (3)
seats in the auditorium thereof.
4. Hotels, motels and other places providing overnight accommodations: One (1) parking space for each room accom-
modation therein and adequate spaces for delivery vehicles.
5. Restaurants: One (1) parking space for each table or booth, and one (1) parking space for every two (2) counter
seats, and adequate spaces for delivery vehicles.
6. Other service and retail establishments: Parking areas, exclusive of driveways, shall be in a minimum ratio of
three (3) square feet of ground area to one (1) square foot of gross floor area, exclusive of storage areas, and adequate
loading spaces.
7. Offices and all other permitted non-residential structures, except agricultural: One (1) space for each one thousand
000) square feet of gross floor area, and loading space for all deliveries and shipping; however, the minimum parking
area shall be in a minimum ratio of one (1) square foot of ground area for each three (3) square feet of floor area.
B. Design of Off-Street Parking and Loading Spaces:
1. For all required off-street parking spaces, open or enclosed, each three hundred (300) square feet of net standing
and maneuvering area shall be considered one (1) space However, if such spaces are for a single or two family dwelling
or are located in a completely enclosed building, each two hundred and fifty (250) square feet of net standing and ma-
neuvering area shall be considered one (1) space. All required parking spaces shall be at least fifteen (15) feet from
- 69 -
===PAGE 72===
street lines and shall be properly maintained in such a manner as to permit them to be used at all times. All required
parking spaces shall be located on the same lot as the use with which such spaces are connected or, in the case of un-
enclosed spaces, within two hundred (200) feet of the lot, except that two (2) or more businesses may jointly provide
the required spaces on one (1) or more of their lots if the lot is within five hundred (500) feet of each of the businesses.
The number of spaces in any such joint facilities shall at least equal the total number required under the provisions of
this section for their individual uses.
2. All open off-street parking and loading areas, permitted and or required, except for single family dwellings, which
are located within or adjacent to a Residence A, Residence B or an Outlying District (whether on the side or rear)
shall be screened from all adjacent lots in said district by strip six (6) feet wide, densely planted with shrubs or trees,
which are at least three (3) feet hight at the time of planting and of type which may be expected to form o year-round
dense screen.
3. Required off-street parking and loading spaces shall not hereafter be reduced, nor shall one be counted as or sub-
stituted for the other.
4. Required -street parking and loading spaces shall have adequate vehicular access to the street, which, along with
the areas themselves, must be approved on a plan in the case of uses in a Commercial District, in accordance with
Section IIIB. Except in the case of parking spaces provided for single family detached or two family dwellings, such
accesses to the street shall not be located closer than two hundred (200) feet apart and shall be provided with separate
entrance and exit lanes.
5. Except in the case of parking spaces provided for dwellings, off-street parking and loading areas shall be paved to
specifications prepared by the Selectmen with the advice of the Planning Board, except that a stand for the sale of
farm products or a farm building may have a gravel surface parking area.
6. Except in the case of parking spaces provided for dwellings, off-street parking and loading areas used after sundown
shall be illuminated, while in use, with illuminations so orranged as not to shine on abutting properties or on streets
in such a manner as to create a nuisance or hazard.
Article 8. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Protective By-law by adding to Section VII-2 the following paragraph:
The Selectmen, or the official or board having the power of enforcing the municipal building By-law, if any, shall take appropri-
ate action in the name of the Town of Lunenburg to prevent, correct, restrain or abate violations of this By-law. Any person
violating any of the provisions of this By-law shall be subject to a fine not exceeding fifty (50) dollars for each offense. Each day
that such violation continues shall constitute a separate offense, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
Article 9. To see if the will vote to amend the Protective by adding after Section I-3, Section I-4 whichwill
add thereto certain new definitions, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY THAT THE PROTECTIVE BY-LAW OF THE
TOWN OF LUNENBURG BE AMENDED BY ADDING AFTER SECTION I-3, A NEW SECTION TOBE KNOWN AS SECTION I-4
WHICH WILL ADD THERETO CERTAIN NEW DEFINITIONS, AS FOLLOWS:
SECTION I-4. DEFINITIONS
For the purposes of this by-law certain terms and words are herein defined as follows: Words used in the present tense include the
future; words in the singular number include the plural and words in the plural number include the singular; the word "shall" is
mandatory and not directory; the word "lot" includes the word "plot"; and the word "land" includes the words "marsh" and"water".
A. Building: A structure having a roof or cover for the shelter, housing or enclosure of persons, animals, or property.
B. Building Lot or Lot: A building lot is that area of land described on a site plan in an application for a building permit
or an application to the Board of Appeals for a permit or a variance, or otherwise defined as the area on which structure is to be
constructed or a use is to be conducted, and which is recorded by planor deed at the Worcester Northern District Registry of Deeds.
A building lot shall not include any part of a street. A corner lot shall be any lot abutting on two (2) or more streets at their
intersection.
C. Dwelling: Any building, or part thereof, used for habitation for one (1) or more persons, but not including commercial
accommodations for transient occupancy or trailers or mobile homes, however mounted.
D. Dwelling Unit: One (1) or more with cooking, living, sanitary and sleeping facilities arranged for the use of one
(1) or more persons living together as a single housekeeping unit, but not more than five (5) persons unrelatedby bloodor marriage.
E. Exterior Line: The dividing line between street and a lot and, in the case of a public way, the street line established
by the public authority laying out the way upon which the lot abuts.
F. Family: One (1) or more persons living together in one dwelling unit, but not including sororities, fraternities and other
communal arrangements.
G. Farm: A parcel of land ten (10) acres or more used for gain in the raising of agricultural products, livestock, poultry
and dairy products, or a parcel with a gross annual value of farm products raised in excess of four hundred dollars ($400.00) It
includes necessary farm structures and the storage of equipment used.
H. Frontage: The linear extent of a lot measured along street. The right-of-way from the intersection of one side lot to
the intersection of the other of the same lot.
1. Garage, Private: Coveredspace for the housing of motor vehicles, a part of or separate from a dwelling but not for the
rental of more than one (1) stall or for commercial repair or commercial storage.
J. Garage, Public: Any garage other than private garage, available to the public, operated by a public authority or for
gain, and which is used for storage, repair, rental, greasing, washing, servicing, adjusting or equipping of automobiles or other
motor vehicles, or supplying of gasoline or oil to motor vehicles or fuel to any kind of self-propelled vehicle.
- 70
===PAGE 73===
K. Gasoline Station/Service Station or Filling Station: An establishment which provides for the servicing of motor vehicles
and operations incidental thereto limited to:
1. Retail sale of gasoline, oil, tires, batteries and new accessories.
2. The changing and repairing of tires (but not including recapping).
3. Battery service, charging and replacement but not including repair or rebuilding.
4, Radiator cleaning and flushing, but not including repair or steam cleaning.
5. Installation of minor accessories.
6. The following operations, if conducted wholly within an enclosed building:
a. Lubrication of motor vehicles;
b. Brake adjustment, replacement of brake cylinders and brake fluid lines;
C. Minor repair including the testing, adjustment and replacement of carburetors, coils, condensers, distributor
caps, fan belts, filters, generators, points, rotors, spark plugs, voltage regulators, fuel pumps, motor hoses,
wheel balancing;
7. Any area of land, including structures thereon, that is used for the sale of gasoline or any other motor vehicle
fuel and oil and other lubricating substances, including any sale of motor vehicle accessories, and which may or
may not include facilities for lubricating, washing or otherwise servicing motor vehicles, but not including the
painting thereof by any means, or major body, engine or transmission work.
L. Guest House, Private: An accessory use on a lot which contains a dwelling. The guest house is to be occupied on a
temporary basis only by non-paying guests of the principal dwelling, and may contain sleepingand sanitary facilitiesbut may not
contain cooking facilities.
M. Hotel, Inn, Motel, Tourist Home or Lodging House: A building, or portion thereof, or a group of buildings on a single
lot, intended to be used for the temporary occupancy of three (3) or more persons who are lodged, with or without meals, and in
which major provisions for cooking may be made in a central kitchen but may not be in the individual rooms or suites.
N. Loading Space, Off-Street: Anoff-street space orberth, on the same lot witha building, for the temporary parking of
vehicles while loading or unloading merchandise or material, and whichhas access to a street or other appropriate means of ingress
and egress.
O. Plan: A plan for a building permit shall containa two dimensional line drawing, drawn to scale, showing the location
and the perimeter outline dimensions of the lot, existing and proposed buildings, and their foundations including exterior stairways
and porches thereon. The plan shall be stampedand signed by a registered land surveyor. The plan shall also show the front, rear
and side yard dimensions from the lot line to the building, the building lot area and the street names.
P. Rooming or Boarding House: A building or premises, other than a hotel, inn, motel, tourist house or lodging house,
where rooms are let and where mealsmay be regularly served by prearrangement for compensation; not open to transient guests; in
contrast to hotels, restaurants, and tourist homes, open to transients.
Q. Sign: Any words, lettering, parts of letters, figures, numerals, phrases, sentences, emblems, devices, designs, trade
names, or trademarks, whether stationary or portable, by which anything is made known, such are used to designate or locate an
individual, a firm, an association, a corporation, a profession, a business, or a commodity or product, which are visible from a
public or private street or right-of-way and used to attract attention.
R. Screened Area: A section shielded from view from adjacent areas by fencing, hedges or trees.
S. Street: A public way or a private way either shown on a plan approved in accordance with the subdivision control law,
or otherwise qualifying a lot for frontage under the subdivision control law.
T. Street Line: Same as Exterior Line. (Clause E.)
U. Structure: Any construction, erection, assemblage or other combination of materials upon the land, necessitating pilings,
footings or a foundation for attachment to the land, excluding fences, but including all indoor or outdoor swimming pools whether
or not so attached and equipment and appurtenances thereto when the pool is used for landscaping, swimming or bathing and has
twenty-four (24) or more inches of depth and has a surface of seventy-five (75) or more square feet,
V. Trailer or Mobile Home: Any vehicle or object whether resting on wheels, jacks or other foundation and having no
motive power of its own, but which is drawn by, or used in connection with, a motor vehicle, and which is so designed and con-
structed as a dwelling unit which permits its transportation and relocation as a complete unit on its own wheels. It shall contain
complete electrical, plumbing, and sanitary facilitiesand may be installed on a temporary or permanent foundation for permanent
living quarters. This shall not include the type of vehicle known as a travel trailer or travel coach nor shall it include any other
prefabricated unit which contains detachable parts equal to or greater than fifty (50) percent of the floor area of the total
dwelling unit.
W. Yard. An open space, other than an enclosed court, on the same lot with a building or group of buildings, which open
space lies between the building or group of buildings and a lot line, and is not occupied or obstructed from the ground upward by
a building or a structure except for fences.
1. Yard, Front: A yard extending across the full width of the lot and lying between the street line of the lot and the
nearest line of the building. The depth of a front yard shall be the minimum distance between the building and front lot line.
2. Yard, Rear: A yard extending across the full width of the lot and lying between the rear lot line of the lot and the
nearest line of the building. The depth of the rear yard shall be the minimum distance between the building and the rear lot line.
3. Yard, Side: A yard between the side lot line of the lot and the nearest line of the building, and extending from the
front yard to the rear yard, or, in the absence of either of such yards, to the front or rear lot lines, as may be. The width of a
side yard shall be the minimum distance between the building and the side lot line.
- 71 -
===PAGE 74===

Extension Service
"An Active Year"
The Lunenburg 4-H Clubs provided an active and stimulating year for the young people of our town. Many
individual and group activities were held.
Again this year the operating clubs were: "Mixed Dairy Club" led by Mrs. Yvonne Bourque, "The Kitchen
Kapers' 'Ied by Mrs. Barbara Raboin, "The Krazy Kitchen Kids led by Mrs. Ruth Anderson, "The Cookie Crumblers"
led by Mrs. Agnes Price, "The Pie Crust Kids" led by Mrs. Marcie Gauntlett and "The Bridle Path 4-H" led by Mrs.
Diane Moore and Mrs. Dixie Beck. The second half of this year showed a decline in three of these clubs.
The year began with individual club work. February brought on monthly bowling fun and our annual bus trip.
This year we went to see the U.S.S. Massachusetts Battleship and toured the Ann Dale Cookie Factory where 4-H
cookies are made. In March a Demonstration and exhibit night was held where area winners went on to county compet-
ition. The winners were Donna Camber, Ed Stockwell and Chris Currie. In April six leaders attended the Annual Leaders'
Banquet held at Camp Marshall. The Horse Club held a Dr. Wright Clinic, had mounted meetings, did barnchecks
and attended an all day clinic in Bolton.
A 4-H Family Picnic ended our official year but not our activities. We dug in and helped out on two clean-up
days. There really wasa lot of rubbish on our streets. The 2nd Annual 4-H Horse Show was held in October under
the direction of Mrs. Lois Camber and turned out to be a great success with Richard Bishop presenting the top 4-H
Awards to the high point winners. The Open Horse Show was held in October under the leadership of Mrs. Camber:
In July we were proud to represent Lunenburg in the Civic Day Parade. A float depicting the three active clubs in
town rode elegantly down Main Street in Fitchburg lowed by a group of horses proudly displaying the 4-H emblem and
colors. Our float took 3rd prize while three of the Horse Club members won trophies for their fine riding.
Approximately twenty-five members attended 4-H Camp this summer. Town camperships were awarded to: Deborah
Kokk, Carol Surrette, Paula Bourque, Patty Cutting and Karen Lindsay. Cookie Sale campership winners were: Donna
Camber, Ed Stockwell and Lisa Surrette. In September the newly elected officers took over. They are Mrs. Ruth
Anderson, Town Chairman; Mrs. Yvonne Bourque, Secretary; Mrs. Marcie Gauntlett, Treasurer. Trustees of the 4-H
funds are Richard L. Bishop and Raymond Proctor. Also in September we participated in the Grange Fair Horse Show
and dairy members exhibited in the dairy show. We also attended 4-H Day at the Eastern States Exposition. A Pot Luck
Supper washeld, slides were shown, and the trophies won at the parade were on display. The Dairy Club went on their
annual Dairy Tour. The Annual Awards Night was held in November with entertainment and exhibits. We ended
the year with Christmas Caroling.
This year saw 4-Hsign erected, on Massachusetts Avenue purchased with funds made available through Susie
Logan, the Worcester County Extension Agent. A new crafts club "The Creative Minds" with twelve members led by
Mrs. Dot Connell was organized. They have been busy making lovely gifts and household articles which they have
had on display. Mrs. Agnes Prices's Club "The Cookie Crumblers" have learned many forms of food preparation and
spent a three day camping trip on which they planned and prepared all their own food.
National 4-H Week was held in the first week of October and was the beginning of our Cookie Sale under the
leadership of Dixie Beck and Dot Connell.
The Bridle Path Club under the leadership of Mrs. Diane Moore and Mrs. Dixie Beck have held many indoor and
outdoor meetings. Each month they have had many activities to keep the club members busy--from clinics to tack
cleaning parties, riding classes, care of horses and record workshops. Helping them out as co-leaders are Miss Sue
Stillman and Peter May. Mrs. Lois Camber is in charge of records and demonstrations. The Junior leaders take charge
of barnchecks and helping the club run smoothly. This club at present has forty-three members and requires a lot of work
and cooperation from both members and parents.
- 83 -
===PAGE 86===
Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical
High School District
Activities began to gain momentum early in the year and culminated in the opening of the school on September
20, 1971. When it became obvious that the construction of the school could not keep to the original timetable, an
"Interim Plan" was developed that would make it possible to open the school within the limitations of the District's
agreement. Hence, an agreement with the City of Fitchburg permitted utilizing the Fitchburg Vocational School as
an "Annex" to the partially completed Technical School.
A cornerstone-laying ceremony on April 25, 1971 heralded the enrolling of students. Congressman Father
Drinan, State Representative Gerald Lombard, Representative George Burke, Monsignor Batista, Representatives of
Labor, our Advisory Committee, and the State Department of Education were among the dignitaries.
Of the 706 applications processed, 662 students were listed in the October Report to the State Department of
Education. This did not include 200 applicants who were placed on a waiting list. Among the tasks that were ac-
complished and necessary prior to the opening of school were staffing, writing a staff handbook, student handbook,
developing a school calendar, master schedule, personnel policy, student policy, equipping, ordering books and supplies,
developing a transportation system, and obtaining approval from the various regulatory agencies. The staff participated
in a 5-week training program, in cooperation with two other Regional Vocational Schools and co-sponsored by the
Network of Innovative Schools and the Department of Education. The workshop developed curriculum, teaching materials,
and teaching methodology.
The third Licensed Practical Nurse graduation was held on September 2, 1971 when 27 graduates received their
diplomas. This was the first class of the newly developed 12-month program as compared to the previous 15-month
program. The curriculum had been approved by the Board of Nurse Registry. The average score this year for our
students is 511 as opposed to 480 a year ago. Mrs. Patricia Traini was named the new Licensed Practical Nurse
Director. A new program, the Rehabilitation Assistant (Occupational, Physical and Speech Therapy Assistant) was
organized in cooperation with Rutland Heights Hospital under the direction of Miss Bessie Wilkin, R.N. In addition,
six Nurse Aide programs were conducted this year at Rutland Heights Hospital under the direction of Mrs. Rachel King.
As a result of the annual survey, nearly all of the LPN and Nurse Aide graduates are employed in hospitals and nursing
homes in the area. Our Advisory Committees continue to function in their appraisals of equipment, staff, and curriculum.
A Certificate of Occupancy was granted to Montachusett Regional for the first four units in September of 1971
and, for the remainder of the building, during the Christmas vacation holidays. The students from the Fitchburg
Vocational School were phased into the remainder of the building when it opened January 3, 1972.
At the regular School Committee Meeting of December 22, 1971, the Committee adopted an Operating and
Capital Budget of $2,858,338 for 1972. The District is entitled to receive 50% reimbursement of all net operating
expenses and 100% reimbursement for all student transportation under Chapter 74. In addition, the State contributes
toward the re-payment of the bond indebtedness for the District. This will continue until expiration of the indebtedness.
Also, P.L. 90-576 monies will be available for constructionand equipment, in addition to specific programs currently
in the school. Finally, member Towns are entitled to 15% reimbursement under Chapter 779 as members in a Regional
District. Such funds are on Chapter 70 entitlement and are directly sent to the Town Treasury.
As this report is being written, Project Proposals pertaining to funding under the Provisions of Vocational
Amendments of 1968 - P.L. 90-576 are being submitted for Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School for
$98,000. In addition, Project Proposals for Titles I and III under N.D.E.A. have also been submitted. To date, the
District has received over One Million Dollars in Federal and State funds; and for this year (1971), the District has
received $456,
Final plans will shortly be submitted to the School Committee for greater utilization of the facilities for up-grading
programs in the afternoons and evenings for adults. Representatives of local industry have made special requests for
programs that will provide new jobs and up-date skills in new technological developments, such as numerical control,
data processing, etc. The evening adult programs will include job preparation and trade extension programs in the
twenty-five (25) skilled areas offered at the school.

Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
It is a privilege to submit my second annual report as Super-
intendent of the Lunenburg Public Schools. This is the 102nd such
report preserved in the Town records and covers the year ending
December 31, 1971.
THE YEAR IN REVIEW -
Lunenburg Schools experienced a year in 1971 that was char-
acterized by definite, observable progress. Although the year was
spiced with controversy surrounding prolonged teacher salary negotia-
tions, a school budget cut of $15,000. in Town Meeting, and a general
tightening of finances toward education (partially created by the loss
of $193,000. in school aid from the State as a result of Lunenburg's
property revaluation), major breakthroughs were made in improving
the school system's communications with the public and in genuinely
William R. Holland
involving parents and other members of the community in our schools.
BA, M Ed., Ed. D
1971 was also characterized by the introduction of several curriculum
Superintendent of Schools
and organizational innovations--innovations that are still in the form-
ative stages and although still needing careful evaluation, are innova-
tions that appear at this point to be very promising and of great value
to students.
IMPROVED COMMUNICATIONS AND COMMUNITY
INVOLVEMENT -
A concerted effort was made this past year to improve commun-
ication between the school system and the community. This effort was
a diversified one and included open houses, a parent survey, increased
newspaper publicity, parent visitation days, a change of school
committee meeting nights, teacher released-time workshops that are
open to the public, the release of preliminary budget figures, and
several other activities that will be unveiled in the spring of 1972.
These attempts have met with considerable success and will be contin-
ued and improved upon in future years.
Good communications also means direct involvement and
understanding. Lunenburg can indeed be proud of the high degree of
Burton E. Goodrich, Jr.
community involvement it enjoys in i ts schools. The best way to show
BSE, ME, M+30
our gratitude for the time and effort given to our schools by parents
Administrative Assistant
and other community members is to publicly identify and thank these
people for their interest and coopération.
Firstly, members of the Music Aiders and Parent-Teacher Organization deserve special thanks for their con-
tinued support in the many school activities they help sponsor and run, whether it be assisting with "Music Man",
the "Pops", serving coffee at a frigid football game, assisting with parent conferences, or knocking on doors to
gather materials for the Christmas Bazaar. These two organizations definitely continued their fine tradition of
success this past year.
In addition to the continued success of the PTO, Music Aiders, Athletic Boosters, and other community
groups that have continually supported the school system's effort, several new forms of parent involvement were

In publicly thanking and identifying a host of people who have volunteered their time and effort in help-
ing improve our schools, one always runs the risk of forgetting someone. Many other parents and townspeople
have supported our school this past year whether it be serving as a guest speaker or donating their services in some
other capacity. Unfortunately, it would be near impossible to enumerate all these people. We do, however,
extend our sincere thanks to all who have assisted US and enthusiastically welcome your future involvement in our
schools. We need your help and cooperation.
NEW "WRINKLES"
Several innovations were introduced in Lunenburg Schools during 1971 in an attempt to refine, update,
and improve the instructional program. Many of these new thrusts were aimed at "what goes on in classrooms"
and reflect careful planning and evaluation by teachers, students, and administrators. We have purposely
avoided adopting the concept of "bandwagonism", i. e., "doing new things simply because other school systems
are doing them".
However, Lunenburg teachers and administrators must be exposed to new curriculum, new ideas, new
research, and must constantly seek self-improvement and renewal. It is also vitally important, if not more impor-
tant, to develop workable strategies for change in Lunenburg Schools which create an atmosphere of openness
among people and provide for careful experimentation. Conflict will never be totally reduced since teachers,
parents, and students have differing philosophies on just what education is or should be in the Lunenburg Public
Schools. Although sometimes painful, a certain degree of conflict is healthy and quite necessary if any genuine
progress is to be made. However, this conflict has to be manageable conflict or else Lunenburg schools run the
risk of moving backwards instead of forward. Helping maintain this balance, this perspective, is not only the job
of a Superintendent of Schools but is the responsibility of everyone associated with the school system.
Enough of the rhetoric and on to the specifics. The following is a brief summary (many of these items have
been explained in detail in our newsletter or in the local newspapers) of some of the more important curriculum
and organizational changes that have occurred this
year:
(1) Lunenburg joined the Merrimack Education
Center and has profited from research and information
services provided by this educational cooperative as
well as the teacher training program and conferences
offered our teachers. As one of twenty school systems
in the Center, Lunenburg also has access to a multi-
plicity of educational resources from universities,
government or industry at a cost far less than it would
be for an individual school system. Because of our in-
volvement with MEC, Arthur D. Little, Inc., selected
Lunenburg as a pilot community for its new pupil enroll-
ment projection service--a service that was completed
for Lunenburg Schools without charge.
(2) The approval of a federal grant last year
enabled US to successfully begin our Work/Study Pro-
gram. Mrs. Frances Gould and Mr. Anthony Falcone
were appointed as work/study field coordinators, ably
assisted by Mr. John Burgess, the Director of the pro-
gram. Twenty students enrolled in the course which
consists of students spending half the school day in the
regular school program and the other part of the day in
a work experience outside the school. Types of work
Gary Johnson, another senior at Lunenburg
positions held by students range from nurses aides, bank
High School in Work/Study Program, busy repair-
clerks and tractor repairmen, to auto parts salesmen,
ing an outboard engine at Glenny's Marina.

and performance are kept and teachers check for
mastery by conducting individual student conferences.
These individualized approaches have great promise and
Mrs. Griffiths, our excellent reading supervisor, is
working closely with teachers as these new materials
and techniques are introduced.
(6) Mr. Paul Romano became our first male
teacher at the third grade level, however, Mr. Romano
was not the only new look at the Elementary Annex.
Phase I of a general upgrading of that plant has been
completed with the correction of some heating, electri-
cal, and acoustical problems. Bright new paint colors,
some new instructional equipment, and new folding
cafeteria tables (in anticipation of a hot lunch program)
have been added. In spite of these first few steps, much
more needs to be done at the Annex if that building is
to be an acceptable educational plant in the years
ahead. With so much uncertainty in regard to future
Individual approach to teaching reading is
growth in Lunenburg, we cannot afford to stop progress
enjoyed by Robert Szocik and Alan Ahti in Mrs.
on the long-range plan to upgrade this 1925 facility.
Karl's 3rd grade class.
(7) Our Learning Disabilities program expertly
guided by Mrs. Frances Bradstreet, received a real
boost with the receipt of a Title II Special Grant of $2500. for the purchase of audio-visual materials to be used
with students who have specific learning disabilities. Lunenburg was one of only 48 school systems in the State to
receive money under this program.
(8) The self-contained classroom and "across-the-board" achievement grouping in all subject areas have
been challenged this year. More flexible forms of student grouping based upon student needs and the individual
talents of teachers have been tried. For example, 2nd grade students were regrouped in two math blocks for the
first time this year. Third grade teachers at the Annex have formed teams with one teacher taking responsibility
for the science instruction of several classes while the other teacher assumes the responsibility for the social science
instruction. In the Middle School, other forms of team teaching, team planning and more flexible student grouping
practices are in evidence.
(9) Mr. Robert Antonucci was appointed as a half-time Assistant Principal at the Middle School. Mr.
Antonucci's leadership has already been positively felt and he has worked very effectively with Mr. Barney and
staff members in initiating many new improvements in the school.
(10) Organizational change was also apparent in the High School program this year. Juniors and Seniors
have a new option in lieu of being assigned to a quiet study area. They now can attend an open study in the cafe-
teria if they so desire. This area serves as an informal lounge and discussion area and is now complete with a
refreshment area and a juke-box (controlled volume type). This privilege has been very responsibly handled by
Juniors and Seniors and has been a credit to the entire student body. Also, Seniors with parental permission and
following certain procedures can leave school early on days when they do not have a last period class. Although
possibly upsetting to some, this increased responsibility given our high school students is very important since stu-
dents today are at the point where they must be given the opportunities to make some of their own decisions, man-
age their own time, and take responsibility for their own learning. Hopefully, greater responsibilities can be
given our High School students in the future; however, this responsibility will be given after there is appropriate
dialogue, planning, and experimentation among all parties. New freedoms won't be given simply because these
freedoms are given in another high school or it looks like the "in-thing" to do. New freedoms will be given be-
cause it makes sense for Lunenburg students and it is the humane thing to do.
- 91 -
===PAGE 94===
(11) High School Curriculum changes have attracted considerable attention this year. New half -year
electives in Consumer Economics and Social Psychology introduced by the Social Studies Department have been
well received by students. The Consumer Economics classes, in particular, have been infused with outside resource
people from the business community and students have received an opportunity via field trips and special projects
to witness first-hand the "world of the consumer". The use of classic feature films on a weekly basis has injected
new life into our non-college English and Social Studies classes. Following a thematic approach teachers from
both academic departments have cooperatively selected these films, arranged the scheduling, and planned the
follow-up activities. A student evaluation of the program revealed positive evidence that this new approach was
achieving better results than conventional teaching methods.
(12) Forty-eight (48) Lunenburg students entered Monty Tech this past fall. Although not all the trade
areas and shops were in operation at the school, the new facility is an impressive one and should serve the voca-
tional needs of certain Lunenburg students.
(13) All staff members participated in a general revamping of the teacher evaluation process. In addition
to making several important revisions in the instruments used to evaluate a teacher's competence andeffectiveness,
several procedural changes were made that resulted in involving teachers more directly in their own evaluation.
Also, Department Heads and Supervisors were asked to play a more direct and active role in evaluating their
teachers. It is now generally understood that in order for a teacher to achieve tenure in Lunenburg he must reach
a higher level of competence than was expected in the past. Improved salary schedules and an increase in the
available supply of outstanding teachers are primarily responsible for increased evaluation standards.
While on the subject of teacher competence, it is interesting to refer to Lunenburg's Annual Report from
the School Committee in 1881-82. This document was given to me by a Lunenburg resident and I read with inter-
est the section of the report that publicly evaluated a first-year teacher named Miss Emma Proctor. The following
is taken from that account:
"Miss Proctor was admirably suited to her employment. Her ingenious selection and arrangement of ques-
tions and method of imparting instruction filled her pupils with a lively enthusiasm, such as we seldom witness in
the school room. As a matter of course she received the hearty cooperation of the parents and the love of her
pupils."
The times have drastically changed, today's students present different problems, and a vast knowledge ex-
plosion has affected US all; however, although the jargon is different, certain comparisons can still be made with
Miss Proctor of 1881. The results of our Parent Survey this past year prove this point--everyone respects teachers
who are committed to their profession and who sincerely like and have a true concern for their students. These
are definitely prerequisites for any good teacher. However, we also should be concerned about a teacher's skill
or "their methods of imparting instruction".
The teachers we hire and retain in Lunenburg must, therefore, possess both the teaching skills and compe-
tencies and the personal qualities that the Town Fathers were talking about when they described Miss Proctor in
1881. Fortunately, in Lunenburg, we have (and have had) many fine teachers who contribute to the outstanding
success and reputation Lunenburg Schools enjoy.
A SUMMARY OF SPACE UTILIZATION
The following is a brief report on how instructional space is being utilized in Lunenburg Schools:
ELEMENTARY SCHOOLS (K-4)
As of October 1, 1971, there were 822 students in Kindergarten through Grade 4. This compares with 833
in 1970, 849 in 1969 and a projected 790 (estimate) in 1972. In other words, enrollment in grades K-4 during the
past three years has decreased by 27 students and in 1972 is expected to decrease by another 32 students. Even
with this decrease, class size in elementary classes will average a desirable 24.5 (estimate) in 1972-73.
All 24 classrooms at the Passios School are being utilized--23 as classrooms and one as a library. All
- 92 -
===PAGE 95===
other small group areas including the auditorium stage are also in use.
At the Elementary Annex, all 7 regular classrooms are being used as well as the teacher's lounge, the
stage and the small downstairs rooms for small group remedial instruction. There is limited square footage and
storage space in five of the regular Annex classrooms making it undesirable to have more than 24 students in these
rooms. As previously mentioned, hopefully, funds will be appropriated this year to continue the 5-year plan to
upgrade the Annex. This plan calls for an annual expenditure of $5000. - $7000. per year between 1971 - 1976.
Phase I of the plan has already been successfully implemented in 1971.
MIDDLE SCHOOL (Grades 5-7) -
Middle School enrollment totaled 551 students on October 1, 1971 compared to 544 in 1970 and 535 in
1969. It is expected that enrollment will reach 570 - 575 students in 1972. Currently, all 24 academic class-
rooms are being utilized as well as the 9 other teaching stations. This building at capacity was built for 600 stu-
dents indicating that we still have some breathing space without being "crammed". Since at this point the addi-
tion of new teaching personnel is not being contemplated for next year, class size is expected to average approx-
imately 28 in the Middle School next year and because of achievement grouping practices in basic skills areas,
class size may be over 30 in some classes. As you can see, the enrollment bulge has shifted from K-4 to Grades
5-7 over the past three years.
HIGH SCHOOL (Grades 8-12) -
High School enrollment totaled 840 students on
October 1, 1971 as compared to 833 in 1970 and 836
in 1969. It is noteworthy that with the loss of over 40
students to Monty Tech, High School enrollment still
&
increased by 7 students in 1971. Anticipating the loss
of another 20 to 25 students to Monty Tech in 1972,
High School enrollment next year is expected to total
847 - 850 students.
All 32 High School classrooms are being uti-
lized along with other teaching stations in special
areas. With some modifications in scheduling short of
Open Campus or an extended school day, the High
School could accommodate another 50-75 students.
However, even with the possibility of accom-
modating additional students in the building, the High
School facility is currently "lacking" in several key
areas. If the High School is to maintain its accredita-
tion in future years and its students are to be provided
with a modern and quality education, immediate
actions should be initiated in the areas of new and/or
Harvard Project Physics--a new highly
renovated science laboratory facilities, career (occu-
motivational, ab-orientated approach to
pational) education facilities, and library/media facil-
physics. (L-R) William Okerman, Glenn
ities.
Stillman, Ed Haka.
The 1968-69 Evaluation of Lunenburg High School done by the NEASSC clearly identified these areas as
areas needing expansion and renovation, and experience in the last two years has added further validity to these
recommendations. Although there are other space needs in the high school (particularly storage of Physical Educa-
tion equipment, guidance counseling areas, a teacher preparation area, etc.), the instructional space needs in
science, career education and library/media areas are the critical ones. The Science Department is working with
a minimum of lab facilities (all science courses are now based on lab-centered, investigative programs and
materials) and a maximum of students. The library continues to limit and turn away students because there is a
- 93 -
===PAGE 96===
lack of seating space as well as other physical restrictions. Career (occupational) education is quickly replacing
the old "general course" curriculum. Although Monty Tech now takes 10% of our students for vocational/technical
courses, there are still approximately 40% of our students who do not complete college and suddenly find them-
selves in the job market without salable skills or even some pre-occupational experiences to use as a basis for
future decisions regarding an occupation. Our limited Industrial Arts course, Business Occupations course, and
budding Work/Study program take up some of the "slack". We need, however, to cooperate with Monty Tech in
order to develop career education programs in our own high school. Half-day, twice a week, or after school,
type occupational education experiences for Lunenburg students using Monty Tech facilities are but a few options
that would allow for maximum use of Monty Tech without asking all students, (I) to make a decision about enter-
ing a trade as 9th graders, or (2) to give up their Lunenburg High School identity for four years.
Secondly, Lunenburg needs to renovate some of its present wasted space (e. g., the old apparatus room
next to the I. A. shops) into Occupational Education facilities. Mr. Goodrich, Mrs. Killeen and Mr. Millea
have been working diligently with State officials on a project to get funding for a graphic communications labora-
tory which would enable Lunenburg High to teach units in lithography, photography, copysetting, bindery opera-
tions, and other areas of graphic communications technology--an occupational need that can be demonstrated in
our employment area.

This stability was aided by the loss of approximately forty students to Monty Tech in 1971. With the possible
loss of another 20-25 students to Monty Tech in 1972, enrollment will still hover around the 2200 figure (see
estimate above).
In August of 1971, Arthur D. Little, Inc., using the modified Cohort Survival Method, completed an en-
rollment forecast for Lunenburg. This forecast was a cautious one since it relied on past historical data and present
statistics and couldn't anticipate the many future variables (e. g. multi-unit housing, regional economic growth,
etc.) that could materially affect Lunenburg's future pupil enrollment.
For example, future predictions using ADL's high estimates, reflected a gradual decrease in enrollment
through 1976 as follows:

Again, these projections do not take into account future variables--important, critical variables that have
a direct bearing upon a residential community like Lunenburg which has considerable property that could be devel-
oped within the next five years.
The major factor that contributed to ADL's sliding predictions was a drop off in the number of actual births
(from an average of 125 births per year (1961 1965) to 96 births per year (1966 1970).
- 94
===PAGE 97===
In summary, it would be safe to say that Lunenburg's pupil enrollment will level off and possibly gradually
decrease during the next few years. I, along with other people knowing the complexion of the community, do
not, however, feel that the decrease will be as great as ADL predicts. The entire projection could also be easily
invalidated by uncertain future housing developments.
SCHOOL COMMITTEE ACTIVITIES
The busy, sometimes hectic, pace set by the School Committee in 1970 continued and in fact, accelerated
in 1971. Considerable time was spent by school committee members in collective bargaining sessions with the
Lunenburg Teachers' Association and the AFL-CIO Cafeteria Union.
Negotiations with teachers continued right through Town Meeting last March and were not concluded
until after mediation and formal fact-finding last June. Because of a projected 30% tax increase and failure to
reach a salary settlement with teachers prior to Town Meeting, voters refused to approve additional budget monies
to cover a possible salary settlement. When a 4.8% salary increase was finally negotiated, the School Committee
found itself with over a $20,000. deficit in their salary budget account. Fortunately, the wage-freeze, the
receipt of additional federal funds, the hiring of eleven first year teachers to replace veteran teachers who left
the system and other economies enabled the School Committee to struggle through the year on the budget sum
appropriated by the Town in March.
Negotiations with the AFL-CIO over a six-month period resulted in the signing in November of a two-
year master contract with cafeteria workers. The contract provides for a salary reopener on March 31, 1972.
Cafeteria workers received a 5% salary increase for 1971 minus the period covered by the wage-freeze.
Currently, the School Committee is negotiating with the Lunenburg Teachers' Association with outside
negotiators serving as spokesman for each group. Both groups are desireous of reaching a salary settlement prior
to Town Meeting; however, collective bargaining is never a predictable business.
In another important decision, the School
Committee agreed to reorganize the non-teaching
areas with Mr. Goodrich, the Administrative Assistant
to the Superintendent, given line responsibility for the
custodial, maintenance, and cafeteria areas. A new
maintenance man, Mr. Irwin Kresser, was also hired
as was Mr. Raymond Lemieux, who replaced Mr.
Archambault, as our Supervisor/Foreman of Buildings
and Grounds. Under the excellent leadership of both
Mr. Goodrich and Mr. Lemieux, Lunenburg now has
its own school maintenance operation--a move that has
increased the general effectiveness of this area and
saved considerable money in our contracted services
account.
In summary, being a school committee member
is a difficult and.many times unpopular job these days.
The dual responsibility of being concerned and respon-
sive to growing financial constraints as well as being
committed to sustaining and improving the educational
program for Lunenburg youngsters is more of a demand-
ing challenge than it has ever been.
It is also important to note that traditionally
"Putting a little light on the subject".

any other financial compensation for their services as
compared with other town officials. Their sincere
opens.
dedication and commitment is many times overlooked

in the emotions of the moment.
Hopefully, the actions set in motion by the landmark California decision, Serrano VS Priest*, will provide
for more equitable ways to support local education costs and bring tax relief to property owners. Such action
will hopefully relieve some of the intense financial pressure felt by School Committees thus allowing everyone to
place greater emphasis on the quality of the educational program in Lunenburg.

JEAN LARKIN and LOUIS FORGUES
After contributing to three very productive and important years in Lunenburg education, Mrs. Jean Larkin
and Mr. Louis Forgues are stepping aside.
Mrs. Larkin has given numerous hours of outstanding service to Lunenburg Schools. She initiated our
school volunteer program, worked with teacher committees in improving teacher lounge and student learning
areas, and has been active in Regional and State School Committee activities, most recently serving on a State
School Committee Task Force on Collective Bargaining. Mrs. Larkin has given of herself above and beyond the
normal duties of a school committee member and her energy and enthusiasm, concern, and deep commitment will
be truly missed.
Lou Forgues leaves the committee after having spent the last year of his term as Chairman. In his letter
announcing his decision not to seek re-election, Mr. Forgues referred to his years on the school committee as
being anything but routine. He referred to the experience as one that makes an individual "more intensely aware
of people, problems and rewards--so much so, that upon leaving, he will find himself a more humble, a more
understanding, and a more humane person. Lou Forgues seriously invested himself in the duties of his office and
carried out his responsibilities admirably.
Besides receiving, both he and Mrs. Larkin gave greatly to Lunenburg during their fenure on the School
Committee.
CONCLUSIONS
In conclusion to this my second annual report as
Superintendent of Schools, I would personally like to
thank principals, teachers, students, school committee
members and members of the non-teaching staff for their
support and dedicated service during the past year. Mr.
Goodrich, my able assistant, Mrs. Welch, my highly
capable secretary and business supervisor, and Mr.
Lemieux, our talented new Supervisor/Foreman of Build-
ings and Grounds, deserve special thanks for the loyal
assistance given me during the year.
Lunenburg Schools are only as good as the people
who run and support them. Fortunately in Lunenburg, we
are blessed with an outstanding school staff that takes
great pride in its many endeavors. We continue to ask
for your confidence and support of our efforts.
DR. WILLIAM R. HOLLAND
Superintendent of Schools
Very pretty, Miss Aija Toini, represents
the Class of 1976 in annual homecoming.

Retirements
MRS. BEATRICE M. BROWN
1945 - 1971
MISS GERTRUDE C. SHEEHAN 1963 - 1971
"Bee", as she is affectionately called by
Miss Sheehan was appointed to the Lunen-
all who know her, retired from the Lunenburg
burg School System in September 1963 as a third
School System in June after completing nearly
twenty-six years in the Lunenburg Schools.
grade teacher where she taught for eight years
After graduating from Framingham State
College, she taught grades one and two for
before retiring in June 1971. Miss Sheehan, a
eleven and a half years in the City of Wey-
graduate of Boston University, was a teacher in
mouth and pre-school and first grade in Colo-
rado Springs for a brief period. She conducted
the primary, intermediate and junior high grades
a kindergarten in her home in Lunenburg for a
in Dedham, Massachusetts, for twenty-five years
period of four years. During her twenty-six
years of tenure as a first grade teacher in Lun-
before coming to Lunenburg.
enburg, she taught in the Town Hall, Center
School, Brooks House and the Thomas C.
Passios Elementary School.
After thirty-three years of dedicated
Her kindness, sense of relative values
service, we all wish her a future of happiness in
and her definiteness of purpose reflected in all
her new home in Centerville on Cape Cod.
those with whom she worked. We all know
that Bee can take great pride in knowing that
she has helped to guide, inspire and develop
the talents of so many young people; but of all
the thanks we can give her, none is so gratify-
ing for her than seeing the fruit of her labors
in the lives of her pupils.
We all wish her many happy and fruitful
years of retirement which she so richly deserves.
- 115 -
===PAGE 118===
JOHN F. HAYNES
1957 - 1971
John became a member of the Custodial Staff
on September 3rd, 1957, when he was hired as Night
Custodian with duties divided between the Jr.-Sr.
High School and the Elementary School. On April Ist,
1961, he was transferred to serve as the Day Custodian
at the Elementary Annex and remained at this school
until the last six months of his employment when he
was transferred to the Turkey Hill Middle School.
From September Ist, 1967 to June 30, 1970, he
served as the School Attendance Officer and was com-
mended for his efforts in performing this duty.
Because of ill health, he found it necessary to
retire effective July 8th. In accepting his retirement
notice, the School Committee expressed appreciation
for the contributions he had made to our schools over
his fourteen years of service. A pleasant and happy
retirement is wished to him by all of US.
Death
MRS. ELIZABETH J. GOULD
1958 - 1966
We were all saddened by the news that "Betty" Gould,
a former teacher and Department Chairman in the Lunen-
burg Jr.-Sr. High School, had passed away after a long
illness on October 13th in Manteca, California. Follow -
ing her graduation from Fitchburg State College in June,
1958, Mrs. Gould began teaching in Lunenburg the follow-
ing September as a teacher of Senior High Social Studies
and English. In September, 1961, she was appointed as
Social Studies Department Chairman and held this position
until June, 1966, when she and her family moved near
Washington, D. C.
Betty Gould was a dedicated teacher and the Class of
1961 upon the occasion of dedicating the 1961 Supplement
of the school yearbook to her, summed up the tremendous
love and affection they had for her:
"This individual has, in our opinion, given not only
her time and patience to the Senior Class, but also herself.
Guiding US through our many activities, during this last
year, she has left with US a feeling of great appreciation
and lasting gratitude. Because of her complete faith and
trust in the Class of 1961, we dedicate this the first sup-
plement "Mother" of the Turkey Hills Echo to our very special
Mrs. Elizabeth J. Gould."

School Nursing Report
for 1971
The Lunenburg Board of Health employs two full-time registered
nurses, Mrs. Doris M. Lahti and Mrs. Gladys V. Barney, and one part-time
registered nurse, Mrs. Shirley M. Carpenter, who works five hours a day only
when schools are in session. The above nurses provide the Town's Visiting
Nursing Service, as well as School Nursing.
A total of 112 visits were made to school children for Physical Defects, Absence from School, and follow-up
on Vision and Hearing Failures. As much as possible, the nurses telephone parents to discuss any problems.
Dr. John DeLeo, School Physician, annually examines students in Grades 4 - 7 - 10, with the exception of
those students who have returned a Physical Form completed and signed by their own doctor. Dr. DeLeo also
examines all school athletes, who also have their blood pressure taken and urine "tes-taped" for sugar.
In March, 1972, we will again offer the German Measles and Mumps Vaccine to certain grades. These
programs and the ones listed below are given through the cooperation of the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health.
Grade 1 only -
Trivalent Oral Polio Series or Polio
Booster for those who have previously
completed the oral series.
Grades 2, 5, 8 -
Dental Fluoride Program givenby
Dental Hygienist.
Grades 1 & 9 -
Tuberculosis Tine Testing with follow-
up on positive reactors.
Grades I & II -
Diphtheria and Tetanus Booster and
Re-vaccination Program.
These programs, which are well accepted, all require permission slips signed by a parent.
Vision and Hearing Testing is done annually on all students in Kindergarten through Grade 12. Notices of
test failures are mailed to parents with a doctor's form attached. This form contains important information on your
child and should be returned to the Nurses' Office after the Ear or Eye docdor has seen the child.
Mrs. Barney attended a series of four all-day meetings on the Handicapped Child in the fall, and Mrs.
Lahti also attended several meetings regarding School Health.
We would like to personally thank the Lions Club for the Massachusetts Vision Tester their organization
purchased when the Middle School opened and also the Kindergarten Slides for the Vision Machine and some other
valuable vision-testing equipment which the Lions purchased for US this year.
The Nurses wish to thank the Superintendent of Schools, the Principals, and all other School Personnel for
their help in making the School Health Program so successful.
Respectfully submitted,
DORIS M. LAHTI, R. N.
- 123 -
===PAGE 126===
School Lunch Program Report
FED
for 1971
My Third Annual Report of the School Lunch Program, covering the
school year September 1970 to September 1971, shows that we served 204, 28I
paid "Type A" Lunches, 17,077 free lunches and 11,066 lunches to adults.
We also sold 36,717 half-pints of milk to students under the Special
Milk Program and gave 6,743 as well as selling 6,698 half-pints to adults. In
all, considering milk given workers, a total of 282,582 half-pints of milk were
purchased and consumed in the 1970-71 school year.
Commodities, bought and distributed by the United States Department of Agriculture, were not as abundant as
in previous years with the total valuation of commodities received in the Lunenburg Schools being $17,868.40.
During the school year we had four teacher released-
time days. On these days, hot lunches to students were not
served; however, the cafeteria personnel took furns serving
a $1.00 buffet luncheon to attending teachers.
We are working on a satellite hot lunch program for the
Elementary Annex. We have served several hot lunches at the
Annex which have been enthusiastically received by both the
third grade students and teachers. At this time, I would like
to thank Mr. Finneron, third grade teachers and the cafeteria
personnel at the Turkey Hill Middle School who helped make
our pilot efforts a real success.
At the Jr.-Sr. High School, the cafeteria personnel
experimented with choice menus which were found to be
quite popular. We also had ala-carte items available on many
days.
Hot Lunch participation this year was 68% in the Jr.-
Sr. High School, 77% in the Turkey Hill Middle School, and
66% in the Thomas Passios Elementary School.
In the one hundred and sixty-nine (169) lunch days we averaged 1,405 "Type A" Lunches per day for all
schools.
In closing, I want to express my thanks to Dr. Holland, our Superintendent of Schools; Mr. Goodrich, our
Administrative Assistant; the School Committee; the Principals and Assistant Principals; Town Personnel; Custodians
and all the School Lunch Personnel, who have cooperated with me to make our School Lunch Program successful.
Respectfully submitted,
LUCY W. CHARTRAND
School Lunch Supervisor
